WOMEN'S GHAT.

MADGE.

many others, evil is wrought by want of thought as well as want of heart." But still it is a question which merits attention, like that other point to which Dr. Murdock drew attention, of the unkindness involved in

hurrying the milliner and dressmaker, which is apt to impose overwork upon the assistants. Early Marriages.—Miss Kathleen Hobbs urged her audience at Manchester—mostly working-class women —not to marry before the age of twenty-five. It has been said that a man,y,hatever his age may be, should

_ marry a woman half iiis age, plus s-even year. This would give the bride of twenty-five a husband of thirty- six, and this could certainly not be _called an early

. naarriage. There has been m progress for some years, a tendency, towards later marriages, especially among the better-to-do classes, and there are several reasons why it should be less rnArked among the working-class population. To the first place a bachelor, whether living by himself or with relatives, is not so comfortable in a house where there are no servants, and the artisan, as a rule, has no servant to wait upon him. Moreover he begins to earn his full wages much earlier in life than the professional man attains to his maximum income and then again the workingches girl loses the charm of youth more quickly than the one who is brought up to a life of ease and luxury. These are some of the chief forces which make for early marriages, but. acting in

the other direction, are -the grea-t-er- facilities -DOW offered, for earning their .own living—which tends to

check hasty marriages probably trueli more effectively than platform homilies. The Low Payment of Wornens' Work.—From an industrial point of view, the prospect of marriage has quite an opposite,influence upon men and women. In the case of the man.it does not alter his career, and is

an inducement to him to _make greater efforts to earn

more wages and succeed in life, but, fcr a woman, marriage, in the. vast majority of cases,alters everything

and the- Prospect that she may only have to temporarily earn her own living, naturally tends to make her slack regarding the conditions of her employment. This explains the reason why women workers are so much less effectively organised than men. The remedy urged

WHICH IS WORSE?

Worry or indigestion? It is a puzzle, but in one respect indigestion is the smaller evil. No amount of effort, or advice, or medicine avails against worry or its causes, but indigestion can be tackled with confi- dence if one goes about it in the right way. It is very true that a stitch in time saves nine, and in the case of some of the more valued recipes a dose in time will save A great amount of suffering. To steer clear of the loss of energy and the absence of pleasure in life and work, which is only the first fruit of indigestion, recourse should be had to a teaspoonful after meals and at bedtime of the following valuable prescription : —Two eunces Glycerine of Pepsine ; ore ounce liarox Compound ; and one ounce Compound Syrup of Hypophosphites. Your chemist will supply any quan- tity in these proportions, or you can mix them at home by sbakino, well in bottle. Try for a fortnight and note result. Cure should te taken to impress upon the chemist the necessity of dispensing this medicine exactly as prescribed.

HUNTER, FIRES INTO DYNAMITE.

South Lorain (Ohio), Sndny,

Every building in the town has been damaged by-the explosion of a dynamite ITlREazine belonging to a local merchant. The windows of all 104 stores and houses were broken, and the chi refi was wrecked. The explosion was caused by a hunter s%ho fired into the magazine, and was himsetf blown to atoms.—Reuter.

LETTER'S LONG JOURNEY.

After sending a letter, posted at Shipley, for delivery at Bingley, Yorks, three miles distant, across the Atlantic to the United States, the Post Officenutborities on delis ering it a month late, demanded 4d. from the indignant address for the extra journey !

The Examiner, Saturday November 2, 1907.

WOMAN'S REALM.

He lies in the adjoining room. Dr. Thorne is there."

"Talbot dead ? " she said. It was hard to believe.

He inclined his head. "Yes." "How—how did it happen ? " she ask. td in a whisper. He shook his head. "I cannot tell you Ido not know. rionly know how they found them. They must have fallen from the viaduct, but Lo v, I cannot say." --Mr. Selby is here," she whispered. "Would —would you like to see him ? " As the Earl made a gesture of indifferent assent, Mr. Selby knocked at the door and entered.

"Forgive me, my lord'," he said, "but I must ask you to come with me. You are a magistrate, and are needed. I have sent a carriage for Lord Saintsbury, another magistrate." The Earl, assisted by Veronica, rose and followed Mr. Selly. They passed the room in which Talbot's shattered body lay, and entercd the dressing-room to which Gibbon had been carried. _ _ _

He, too, had been terribly shattered by the fall, but, strange to say, was still alive; and he lay with his bruised and battered face half covered with bandages and E o deathlike that but for the occasional flickering of his eyelids he had all the appearance of a corpse. Dr. Thorne, who was kneeling beside him administering restoratives, looked up and uttered a sigh of relief.

"Glad you've come, my lord," he said, in a hushed voice. "He is alive still. 'He has spoken —just a word. The spine it is a marvel that he should have lived. He cannot last long. Something seems to worry him, to be on his mind."

They looked down at the death-stricken man with silent awe. It was evident that his mind was not at peace,-for he moved his head from time to time restlessly, and his lips opened, but only with a moan. After awhile Lord Saintsbury was admitted, went to the Earl's side, and watched like the rest. _ _ _

Suddenly Gibbon's eyes opened and, drawing a long breath, he looked round. "The Earl--a magistrate !" he gasped. Lord Saintsbury came to the bedside and bent down. "The Earl is here. lam a magistrate, too. What is it you want to say, my poor fellow ? " he asked.

Gibbon tried to raise himself, but, of course, could not do so. "He—he did it," he said, in a voice strangely mechanical and calm, indeed almost indifferent. "Put—put me on my oath; there.'s—there's witnesses here, aren't they ? " "Administer the oath," said Mr. Selby, in a low and eager voice. "For Heaven's sake be quick, my lord! You do not know how much depends----" The oath was administered, and after a painful effort the dying man managed to gasp out in broken sentences, which would have been inaudible if Lord Saintsbury had not knelt beside him and almost put his ear to the bleeding lips: "He did it—my master, Mr. Talbot. I followed him to the woods and saw him try and steal the pocket-book—" "Pocket-book? What pocket-book ? " muttered Mr. Selby to himself as he bent over the dying man.

"They fought for it, and Mr. Talbot 'stabbed him. Then, when he'd gone for the spade—I— took the pocket-book from the body. It's—it's —feel in the breast-pocket of my coat." Mr. Selby felt and clutched the precious object, and Gibbon drew a long breath, as if of relief.

"I've—l've carried it there ever since. I—l know why he did it. I've read the certificates. He—he knew Oa Ralph Farringdon was the Earl's son. He—he wanted to rob him. But " 'with a twist of the battered lips—"l—l spoilt his game. You'll—you'll find his clothes, his dress clothes, in my box. They're all stained and soiled with the mould. He gave 'em to me the—the fool !—and they're just as—as he left 'em." _ _ _

His voice died away, and he struggled for breath; then suddenly he went through the awful scene on the viaduct, repeating almost every word that lmd passed as if he and Talbot were still face to face.

" ' Beast ' 'drunken beast dog cur ' ! He always treated me like that. I was like the dirt under his feet. hated him—hated him! He used to steal out in an old coat to—to Isaac's gambling place. He—he was a bad lot, a thorough bad lot. Worse than me, gaol—gaol- bird as I was ! " .

There was silence for a moment or two, in which the horrified group about brill avoided each other's eyes; then the broken, gasping voice went on:

"Ah,,would you! I can see it in your face! There's murder there, as there was the night you killed the man! But I'm as strong as you Mr. Talbot! You can't force me over. It's death, death, death! But I'm strong. Ah, Heavens, I'm going! But not alone. No, no! If I go you shall go with met I'll held you! Ah, we're over!

• He tried to rise; his arms were flung out and embraced the empty air with a spasmodic, an awful gesture. The white face was convulsed with a hideous hate and almost as hideous a terror, and when the arms fell they still seemed to strain at some object between them. With a last effort the wretched man raised his head and; staring straight before him, gasped : "Together—yes, together! You shan't escape No, no! I'll hang you—hang you!" The terrible voice stopped suddenly. Dr. Thorne looked up and quickly drew the sheet over the wreck of a face.

- "lt is all over," he said. The Earl drew Veronica to him. She was shaking, and moaning all unconsciously. "It is all over," repeated Dr. Thorne, rather quaveringly. "The man is dead." "And Lord Denby is cleared," said Mr. Selby in a low voice, which, for all its gravity, had a note of solemn exultation, as he watched Lord Saintsbury close his note-book,

CHAPTER XXXIII. An enormous crowd collected in the market- place at Halsery, and some time before the hour op which the court opened converged on the court-house. The news of the death of Talbot Denby and his valet Gibbon, and the rumour that the latter had confessed to being an eye-witness of his master's crime, had been Started almost as soon as Gibbon's last words had been gasped out, and the rumour, confirmed by servants from Lynne Court, having been strengthened into "reliable intelligence," the populace, already intensely interested in Ralph Farringdon and his fate, were all ablaze with excitement. As they surged before the court-house and pressed against the heavy old doors they shouted and yelled, and there were not a few voices actually clamouring for Ralph's release. The court was to sit at eleven, and as the hands of the clock were nearly upon the hour a cry rose from the mob, for the Lynne Court carriage was seen coming along the High-street. In it were seated the Earl ,and'Veronica. It was noticed by the eager mob that they were both in mourning,.and that Lord Lynborough was deathly pale. Veronica, too was pale, but a light shone in her eyes which .the crowd that thronged round the carriage was quick to interpret. Someone started a cheer, but it was promptly hushed by those who remembered that at Lynne Court lay the dead body of the man whom they had all learnt to regard as its future master. The Earl and Veronica entered by the magistrates' door, and soon afterwards the public were admitted, and the small court was instantly crammed by as many of the excited- and eager mob as could squeeze into it. All the magistrates were on the Bench, excepting Lord Saintsbury, who stood in the body of the court talking to Mr. Selby, upon whom all eyes were centred. Veronica went up to Mr. Selby and held out her hands, both hands indeed. "Does—does he know..?" she whispered, her lovely face all aglow. "Have you told him? What have you told him? They would not let me see him last night or this morning." Mr. Selby nodded. "Forgive me, but that was by my orders—or advice rather. I feared that you would tell him that his innocence had been proved." "As it has ! " she breathed. "Oh, as it has ! " "Forgive mo, Miss Veronica—not yet. There is many a slip 'twixt the cup and the lip--" "()h, Mr. Selby, a proverb at such a moment ! " she breathed with piteous. reproach.

"I know, I know; but we lawyers muse be careful. Until the Bench has heard the evidence I would not risk telling Raluh—Lord Denby— that he was sate. but I told him enough to give him hope, though I need scarcely have done that. Have you noticed—what •a noise they make, and how excited they aro ! " he broke off, Looking round at the seething crowd; "I can

scarcely make myselr heard—have you noticed that he has been the most hopeful, the most confident of all of us ? " "Is there anything I haven't noticed ! " she . said in a low voice. 'lt is because he knew that the truth would pre--" "Oh, proverbs at such a moment!" he murmured, smiling, and with a very fair imitation ' of her voice. "You see, I'm so happy that I can joke, MieeVeronica—" "Silence ! " called the clerk. Veronica stole back to her place beside the Earl, who sat with his hands resting on his stick, his white head bent as if he were unconscious of the crowd, the noise, and the excitement. But as the prisoner was ushered into the dock the old man raised his head and looked—with what love and remorse, and pride !—at the handsome face, the "clear' just" eyes. For the first time since his arrest Ralph's face looked strained, as if he were feeling the suspense, the delay. His eyes went from his father to Veronica, and something in hers made his own flash and his face glow with colour. The crowd was not stilled into silence until , Mr. Selby rose, then you could have heard a pin drop—if it could have dropped between the I packed people. He was almost as pale as the prisoner had been when he entered the court, ' and for the first time his usual calm and steady voice wavered. "Your worships," he said, "when the court adjourned yesterday I was calling as a witness Mr. Talbot Denby." He paused a moment, then went on with deep solemnity. "He could not be found. Since then he has been summoned to another Tribunal and has appeared before another Judge. He has quitted this mortal scene, and with his dis- appearance my task is over." Ralph uttered a faint cry. "I leave the case from this moment in the hands of my friend Mr. Graham, who represents the. Crown, and, I may add, the interests of justice." He sat down, the crowd moved restlessly and impatiently, and a man - was heard to say com- plainingly: "Why doan't 'cc let 'im free to oncet Mr. Graham rose: He, too, was pale and very grave. "Your worships," he said, "I am grateful to Mr. Selby for leaving to me the task of clearing up the mystery of this painful case. I will not take up the time of the court by addressing you, but will call—" "Let him free, let him free ! " shouted the crowd, but it was quieted by the clerk and the police, and one after another the witnesses—the boy who found the bodies, and Dr. Thorne, who proved the death of master and man—passed through the witness-box. Then, as the crowd grew almost unmanageable, Lord Saintsbury entered, and in low, but clear tones read Gibbon's confession. I it was listened to with an almost intolerable impatience, the people looking eagerly from Ralph, who stood gripping the edge of the dock, to the Earl and Veronica. "Nough said ! " shouted a woman. "Let 'un go! Let the young lord free; he've been there in that shameful place long enow !" !i Old Lord Sebrow, who acted as chairman of the Bench that morning, held up his hand, and at length succeeded in obtaining silence. "The Crown withdraws the charge, Mr. Graham ? " i "Yes, my lord, with sincere regret that it should have been made." "Silence,_ please! Ralph Farringdon," said Lord Sebrow. "Lord Denby, Lord Denby ! " shouted the crowd. I "Ralph Farringdon, otherwise Lord Denby," said the old lord, making himself heard with difficulty, "it is our pleasant duty to tell you that we find the charge made against you is not proven; in fact,- we know that you are innocent of the crime of which you are accused, and we discharge you. You leave this court with a character unstained as far as this case is concerned, and we echo the regret expressed by the prosecutor for the Crown." Ralph bowed liis head. "I thank you, my lord,' he said in a low but clear voice; and his eyes went straight to Veronica. • His words had been almost drowned by the roar of the crowd, which, disregarding the injunetiont of the police, now swayed towards the dock with outstretched hands. One or two of the persons nearest to him had seized his arms, and before they could be prevented, had lifted him bodily from the shameful place, and, setting him on their shoulders, bore him into the open air. I Hero they were received by the mass of people who had been unable to get into the court, with an outburst of cheering that was well-nigh deafening. The Lynne carriage was drawn up • opposite the magistrates' entrance, and the "iiersons—there were women amongst, them—who • were carrying Ralph placed him in it, and the mob pressing round it threw up their caps and shouted triumphantly. No doubt not a few of them forgot that he was no longer Ralph Farringdon the gamekeeper, "one of ourselves," as they put it, and were under the impression that his acquittal and liberation represented a triumph of the "masses" over the "classes." Another reason for their excessive joy may be . found in the fact that Ralph had been a favourite with all—excepting the poachers—and , that before the charge of murder he had already I been a popular hero in consequence of his rescue of Ada. I "Thank you, thank you!" he said. "I'm as glad as you are that I've been cleared of this elarge." 1 "Aye, cleared, sure enow," said a voice indignantly. "But how would it a-been if I Vogler as did it had na met his fele ! " I The words struck a note that the speaker had not contemplated, for they recalled the fact that I Ralph's cousin, the nephew of the old man who was just then coming towards them, lay dead at Lynne Court. A silence fell as suddenly as that when a mill wheel stops. A Hand had been , stretched out from the blue ether to snatch • Ralph from mortal peril, but at the same time It I had dealt out to the guilty an awe-inspiring punishment. So, in comparative silence, they made way for the Earl and Veroniett. Ralph leaped from the carriage and clasped his 'father by both hands, and they looked into each other's eyes for a moment; then Ralph took Veronica's hand and raised it to his lips. I At this the enthusiasm of the crowd, which had not been extinguished but was only smouldering, blazed up again, and a cry of sympathy ran through the serried mass. Ralph assisted the Earl and Veronica into the carriage, I and the old man stood erect—a striking figure at I that electric moment—and held up his hand. There were many there who had borne the great Lord Lynborough no great goodwill. He had been a hard, if a just, landlord. He had never shewn the least sympathy with the people on his lands' the stranger within his gates. He had lived the life of a morose recluse. But as they stared at him they were one and all conscious that the man—old as he was—had changed. The austere face had softened, the hard eyes were aglow with a novel sympathy, the once haughty frown had gone, and the thin lips, which many had never seen but with a cynical smile, quivered with emotion. They moved once or twice as if he were speaking, but the words were inaudible, but at last those near him heard the murmur: "My friends—thank God with me—my son was lost and is found. My son—my dear son." As he turned away to hide the tears that did not shame his manhood but proved it, his baud sought Ralph's and held it.

• When Ralph and Veronica came back from their honeymoon—they had been married three months after the day of his acquittal—there were, of course, great doings at Lynne Court. It shall be left to the local newspapers to describe those doings, and extremely well the local newspapers accomplished their pleasant task; but it may be allowed him who has set down this much of the history of Veronica and Ralph Denby, to recount one little scene which occurred on the night—the culminating night—of the prolonged festivities.

The people, gentle and simple—and they mixed as one class on this occasion—had gone, and Veronica had thrown herself upon the settle before the blazing tire in the hall. It was winter when they returned from their honey- moon. Ada. who was almost a permanent visitor

USEFUL COSTUME FOR COUNTRY WEAR

Of a very smart and useful type is the becom- ing walking costume pictured in our sketch. Just the thing for regular country walking or driving wear, it is equally suitable for mornirrs shopping or every-day use in town. The mate- rial in which it is realised is a firm, heavy clot': of a very pretty dark blue shade, a material which will stand any amount 'of wear and tear and yet contrive to look smart and nice. From. the neck of the hip-long coat turn back revere

and collar of black astrakhan, the same service- able fur being used for the, gauntlet cuffs on the plain sleeves. The coat fastens down the front in single-breasted fashion with four large but- tons, a line of machining running down the edge on either side and being continued round the bottom. A row of machining also outlines the long breast seams, which add greatly to the "bang" and general effect of the coat. The skirt, which is cut of sensible walking length, is box-pleated all round, the pleats being firmly machined down for about a third of their length and then released.

FASHIONS IN THE HUNTING FIELD

In the matter of fashion there is little to re- count as regards dress for the hunting field, says a Globe fashion writer. It is changed from season to season solely in tiny details, observed only by the initiated. The one great change noticeable by all is the increased wear of the divided skirt, its open advertisement by the well known habit makers, and the advance that is be- ing gained inch by inch each season by the astride position in the saddle for our sex. There is no difference in the length of skirt, and there is no patent for safety more depended upon than the apron-cut, which is now so generally used for hunting. How anyone can think it more modest and womanly than the divided skirt we cannot imagine. Even among the women who ride astride there is tome difference of opinion as to the neatest and best style of clothes. Several believe in riding breeches, top-boots, and a long loose skirted coat hanging well over the knee at either side. Wearing of veils by hunting women is now much more general than it was. pis is because of the difficulty of keep- ing hair sightly. It is not a fashion to be en- couraged; fancy a man riding to hounds with a veil on! If such things must be worn, they should be of clearest mesh, strong, comfortably and tautly fixed,- with never a spot about them.

===== 0002601_19071102_art0020.txt | included=true | included =====
JAPANESE SILK BLOUSE.

Materials may come and go, but Jap silk re- mains perennially popular and fashionable. It is precisely in this most useful of materials that the charming blouse pictured in our sketch is realised. The upper part is cut in the form of a deep yoke, pointed in front, and slightly rounded over the shoulders. This yoke is edged by a wide Valenciennes insertion of rather heavy make, above which comes a row of spots embroi- dered in ivory washing silk. Above again comes

AA

a second row of insertion and another row of embroidered spots, the yoke being completed by a neckband of insertion and embroidered silk. Just in the middle of the front, below the point, comes a sort of tab or exten- sion of the yoke, which is made of insertion and the spot-embroidered. silk. The fulness of the material is set on to the yoke and tab in a series of short tucks, which are released in a line sloping from either side towards the small centre box-pleat, which is continued right to the waist. The sleeves are of the new shape —full at the shoulder and fitting closely below the elbow, the .back of the arm being finished with a row of embroidered buttons. HATS OF THE MOMENT.

Those that we see mostly in London now, as the result of the autumn purchases, are of good size and breadth, says the Queen, and horn them hang a perfect forest of feathers that have a most comical effect. Flowers and feathers alike are being dyed in tones from red to mauve, but white ones seem to have the preference over any the paradise plumes, which people are wear- ing of enormous size, fluttering from the side; caught in a breeze they assume, most varying aspects. Covered hats find some favour; moire, velvet, and cloth, but they nearly all have a gigantic pouf of feathers, and some of the khaki shades aro a feature in fashions. Stiff aigrettes rise from the centre of many of these headdresses, but in Paris smaller sizes arc preparing, and though an abundance of feathers are introduced, they are laid flat. The mousquetaire is a favour- ite shape in felt, with high crown and undulating brim.

TO-IJAY'S RECIPE.

MACARONI EGGS.—First make a thick NOIRE) sauce with half-a-pint of milk and a tablespoon- ful each of butter and flour. Season with pepper, Salt, and a few drops of tarragon vinegar; stir while it boils. Cook some large macaroni in salted water, drain very dry, and mix with half the white .sauce and a teaspoonful of chopped parsley. Have ready some grated ham or tongue. Butter a pie-dish and in it place a layer of maca- roai and sauce, then a layer of the tongue or Pam. riace on tnis as many raw eggs as your dish will hold. Over the eggs place another layer of grated tongue and fill up with macaroni. Cover the whole with the remainder of the white sauoe. Bake till the eggs are set.

Mr. Selby nodded. "Yes. In my opinion he man who killed James Oatway was Iliss Veronica, I have warned-you ; you will be calm? —is "—his voice dropped—" Mr. Talbot Denby ! " Nowithstanding the .warning, Veronica started, and a cry. of horror and incredulity escaped her. "Talbot!" she said. ,"Oh, no, no! It is impossible !" .„ "Nothing is so possible as the impossible," he said; ' "nothing is so - certain to occur as the unexpected. That is a truism which is brought home to us lawyers every day in the week. Listen. My suspicions were first koused by the fact that Mr. Denby had been ou .in the woods that night, and that he had—denied it." "He wanted to avoid being mixed up with the ease," murmured Veronica. "Besides, what motive could Talbot have in—in killing the man? What connection was there between them ? "

"The first question I can't answer definitely, though I can give a tolerable guess; the other I can. Look at this." He took a silver match- box from his pocket. "If you remember, this was found on the body. At the time no one thought anything about it. It is true that it has' the Denby crest, but the man might have picked it up, or stolen it. "Yes, yes! " she assented, eagerly. "Oh, yea!" " Yes ; but, on the other hand, it may have been given him by Mr. Talbot Denby. Anyway, Groser, to whom I shewed it just now, says that he noticed it in Oatway's hand the day of the murder, and that he had not seen it in his possession before then; and he could not have failed to observe it if Oatway had possessed it, used it. Now, there is the connection between the two. It is a small, almost invisible link; but it is a link, and I think I can construct the rest of the drama from it."

"How ? " aslod Mr. Saintsbury. "By Mr. Denby's aid," replied Mr. Selby. "I am going to ask him a few questions. There are several ways of asking questions, but I know of one—it is common property to us lawyers—by which the interrogated is led on to answer more than he is asked. For instance, I shall ask Mr. Talbot if he minds my smoking, and shall borrow a match. He will not have one, or he will bring out a brand-new match-box. I shall admire it, remark on its newness, and he will volunteer the statement that he bought it to replace an old one which he lost—he will say a long while ago. Then—" he shrugged his shoulders—"well, I shall forge that chain, Miss Veronica. I am going to the Court to see Mr. Talbot. You will say nothing to Mr. Ralph—beg pardon—Lord Denby ? " He had himself driven to Lynne Court and inquired for Mr. Denby. "Mr. Denby went by the 4.30. He was called to town on important business, sir," said the butler.

"Bad news ? " asked Mr. Selby. "What a beautiful place you have here. Great responsibility for you! I hope it wasn't bad news?" The butler shook his head. "I'm afraid that there—there was something wrong, sir," he said, gravely. "I heard his- lordship the Earl and Mr. Denby talking, you might say quarrelling, in the library." Mr. Selby drove back to Halsery and got hold of Mr. Grey, and they went to the station. The sleepy-headed porter said that, yes, Mr. Gibbon and Mr. Denby—he put Gibbon first—had driven up to the station, and that they'd gone by the 4.30. Be hadn't seen them get into the train, but Mr. Gibbon had taken the tickets, and, of Eourse' they wouldn't have taken tickets if they hadn't wanted to go. , Selby drew Grey aside.-7" Wire to Scotland -Yard and tell them to keep an eye on Mr. Talbot Denby," he said. Grey started. "Surely you don't suspect---" he exclaimed; Init at last Mr: Selby got the wire sent off. Then he went back to Halsery, and, shutting himself up in his room, went Over the ease point by point. Presently there came a knock at the door and, thinking it was the servant come to tell him that dinner was ready, he called out: "Don't want any dinner. Go away!"

But the door opened and Grey came in. He was pale and excited, and so breathless that he could scarcely speak. . ... . ... .

" There' s—ihe're' s been an accident ! " he stammered.

Mr. Selby frowned ;impatiently. What were accidents to him, engrassed in the case? 'All right. Send for the doctor. What do you bother me for! Any anrer from Scotland Yard yet ?"

Grey shook his head desperately. "No need to wait for that, Mr. Selby. The accident's happened to Mr. Talbot Denby ! " Mr. Selby sprang to his feet all alert and interested. "Mr. Denby ? " "Yes, •he was found lyinc, in the coombe below the viaduct. Geordy Ifilson's boy found them when he was fetching the cows home." " Them ? " queried Mr. Selby. "Yes, his man, his valet, Gibbon, was lying beside hiim. In fact, ,they were lying gripping each other as if—as If they'd been struggling or caught at one another as they fell,"

Mr. Selby was relching down his hat.„"Whero are they ? " he asked. "At Lynne Court," replied Grey. "The boy was frightened—as well he might be !—and ran home screaming. He met some men, and, rccognising Mr. Talbot Denby, they carried both to Lynne Court."

"Get a fly," said Mr. Selby. "I've got one ready." "Are they dead P" a3ked Mr. Selby as the started.

Grey shook his-_head. "I can't say; I didn't stop to inquire, but came to you at-once." Quite right! Hi, stop!" They were opposite the Roebuick. _ _

He ran in and found Veronica. She had heard the news, and was pale and oppressed, by tha tragedy. You will come with me ? "..he said. Earl will want you." For answer she went upstairs, and returned to him in a few minutes with her hat and jacket on. Little was said dining the drive. They found the Court in a. state of excitement—servants standing in groups in the hall, the butler moving about as if he were unconscious of his actions. "The Earl ?" were Veronica's first words.

at tire -Court, was tying on the bearmin on tar hearth. She and Martha had joined in everythln6 that day, and the child had cuite eihaustea herself and was fast mice') with her bead resting Igainst Ralph—Lord Big Man she called him now —and for the first time for several days quiet reigned in the vast house. Veronica leant forward, her hands clasped round her knees, her eyes gazing dreamily at the huge log whose rays lit up her lovely face With a rosy hue. . _

. . "Tired, dearest ? " said Ralph in a low voice, so that he should not wake the sleeping Ada. "No, only a wee bit. I was thinking." "Of what ?" he asked. "Not sad thoughts,. Veronica ? " . _

She raised her lovely eyes and looked at him- with the look that never failed to thrill him,, • then her expression grew a little piteous. "Must I tell you, Ralph ? " she pleaded. "Of course," he responded, mercilessly.;. "You belong to me, thoughts and all, dearest.' "Yes " she assented, and she stretched out her hand. He took it and kissed it and still held it as she said slowly, almost reluctantly, and in ai low voice that is, and always will be, the sweetest music he can hear: . "If I must, Ralph; I was thinking that perhaps, after all, should have liked it better.— Ah, no, I won't say it! It seemi ungrateful, childish." "Speak, I command you!" he murmured, with mock solemnity. "I was thinking how—it would have been—if you you will laugh at me, Ralph! you always do, you know, when I want to be very serious," she broke of?, with the air of a martyr. He shook his head, and laughed softly. But the laugh, instead of discouraging, impelled her; "If you had remained just Ralph Farringdon, if you had gone to Australia, and I had followed you—l should have followed you, Ralph! Nothing, nothing would have prevented me, you know." He nodded. "I can well believe it; there never was half so stubborn a person as Miss Veronica Denby when she had made up her mind."

"Not always, Ralph," she murmured, remorse- fully. "You forget. I yielded once. I let you go —that day in the arbour." "The one exception that proves the rule," he said. "But you soon repented, dearest." "Yes," she admitted with a sigh of satisfac- tion. "I soon repented. I did follow you, Ralph! "—piteously. "But suppose "—she rose and stole to his side and hid her face against his breast—" suppose we had been married—Ws should have been married, Ralph ? " "Without a doubt, I should say," he returned with a smile that only masked his love and pride. "You are the sort of young person who will have her way at all costs.'

"And we had been very poor, and had had a struggle, you and 1, just Mr. and Mrs. Farringdon,' do you think you would have—have care 4 more for me? Oh, wait! Sometimes I think Heaven has been too good to me, made it too—too easy for me. I gave up nothing for you. lam back here "—she looked round the hall - almost regretfully—"l shall reign—not for a long, long time, I hope—where once I served. I have gained everything by my love for you; and—and sometimes—to-night, for instance, now that they have all gone—l think that I should have liked— that it would have been very precious to have sacrificed something for you, to have worked with you for our daily bread, to have starved for. you—ah, Ralph, that would have been sweet to me !—but Providence laughed at me, and all ray dreams of sacrifice and self-denial. You have given me so much—everything !—and I, Ralph, have, after all, given you nothing." He set the child's head gently down on the soft bearskin, and, rising, took Veronica's hand and led her up to the antique mirror gleaming in one of the oaken panels. "Look, dearest," he whispered, and he made' her look at the lovely, blushing face reflected there. "That is what you have given me. De you count it nothing? To me it is the greatest prize, the most precious treasure—" A step sounded in the hall behind them, an the Earl, leaning on his stick, came forvilbra slowly. "They have all gone? Ralph! Veronica I. where are you ? " She turned from Ralph, and putting her arms round the old man's neck kissed him.

He looked down at her tenderly, fondly, then ae said in the voice that had grown so soft and gentle: "Tired, my dear? Why—why—you are crying;, Ralph! What are you crying for, Veronica? She forced a smile as her violet eyes looked from one to the other. "For sheer happiness," she whispered.

He was not quoting anything said by Mr. Birrell or Mr. McKenna, he did not know what the Bill next year would contain, but he knew on very good authority indeed one of the reasons which weighed —not with the great body of Liberal Churchmen who would have liked to see the Bill through—but with the small set of men of very extreme views in the Church of England whose views had been apparent in dealing with the Deceased Wife's Sister Bill as well as educa- tion. (Hear, hear.) The offer made by Lord Crewe and Mr. Birrell was as generous and considerate as any statesmen could possibly have made. (Applause.) Mr. CROSFIELD then went on to refer to the burning question of Socialism. Mr. Bell's answer to Lord Claude Hamilton's manifesto was a very proper answer, he thought, because Mr. Bell told Lord Claud straight that if he commenced to tackle Socialism the simple answer was that be himself was merely doing his utmost to manufacture Socialism. (Hear, hear.) At the Junior Liberal meeting, on Friday he mentioned a little inci- dent which occurred in London when he discussed the question with Socialists who were great friends of his. Mr. Crosfield theftroceeded to repeat the conversation which ensued with reference to the relation of Socialism and art under a communistic regime. In conclusion Mr. Crosfield thanked his hearers for the splendid way in which all had pulled together and fought the last general election. They had to remember that in the Liberal party there were innumerable shades and degrees of opinion. They recognised that at the last election. But there was no fighting under false colours. The case was made clear, they said what they were going to do, and they had tried to carry out their promises. (Applause.) Having won that great victory and knowing that they had now time to make good their position let them proceed to do so and be ready when the next attack came, to keep the Liberal colours flying in Warrington. MR: TONIKINSON- ON LAND REFORM.

Mr. TOMKINSON, M.P., who had a hearty reception, referred to his previous visit to Warrington many years ago in company with a great statesman of that day, a leader of the progressive forces, and on the eve of a general election. What a change there was now. He congratulated Mr. Crosfield on his proud position as member for Warrington (Applause). ,Mr. Crosfielthhad touched upon some of the work which the Liberal party had done. He (the speaker) submitted that both the present House of Commons and the Ministry had deserved well of the country. The Ministry was an earnest, energetic, and capable one which had honestly endeavoured to carry out all the pledges on which it was returned so far as the time which had elapsed rendered it possible. (Hear, hear.) The three great watchwords of Liberalism bad beert ever to the fora_ Po a abroad and the grant of self-government to our South African colony had been secured. Sir Edward Grey andi the King himself both contributed to this happy condition of affairs. The Government had also remedied taxation, taken off the coal duty, taken a penny off tea and 3d. in the £ off the income tax of people earning under £2,000 per year, while an increased tax had been placed upon the fortunes left by people at death. (Applause.) So much for place and retrenchment. He remembered that, on moving the address to the Throne in the House last Febraary, he said that the list of legisative measures was a very long one, though not greatly longer than that of the previous year. The address was swollen by more than one private member's Bill which had been adopted by the Government. Measures had been dealt with by infinite pains and sent on their journey to "another place." How to curtail the power of the House of Lords was a great question. The will of the House of Commons must prevail- within the limits of a single Parliament. (Hear, hear and applause.) That was one question to which they had to apply themselves. (Hear, hear.) Mr. Tomkinson then went on to speak of the

LAND TENURE BILL. Let it not be thought, he said, that the land question was not of vital importance to the inhabitants of the towns. It was of the greatest importance to the com- munity in general. The Land Tenure Bill gave charter to farmers. It had given them ample and fall security for good farming, and for all the improve- ment they made on their farms so long as the improve- ments increased the value of the farm. Before that, if the tenant did •not get the land- lord's uermission in writing:" there was no security for his improvements. He could not now be turned out of his farm without payment for his improvements and compensation for disturbance. (Applause.) That was a great security to the tenants. (Hear, hear.) They had given the tenant freedom to buy what he liked and sell what he liked, and he could only he penalised on leaving the farm to the extent by which any injudicious cultivation on his part might have damaged the farm. He was also given security for the ravages of ground game, and had a full claim upon his own landlord for any damage thus sustained. (Applause.) Mr. Tomkin- son then proceeded to discuss THE SMALL HOLDINGS ACT.

Tories often said it was a mistake to suppose that neople wanted to live in the country; they went to the towns because there was more amusement. From a long experience he (Mr. Tomkinson) believed that to be untrue. Mr. Tomkinson gave an instance of an offer of small hold ings,in -which he was concerned,which resulted in the receipt of 1700 applications for holdings in a few months. The great curse of agriculture hitherto had been the merging of three or four farms low one— which he considered was a wicked thing to do. They wanted now to turn back the hands of the clock and return to the natural condition of things which sent the first man into a garden to till it. He believed that if people were given a chance of, settling on the soil they would GLADLY A.VAIL THEMSELVES of it. The ideal system was that which largely obtained in Cheshire and which. enabled the industrious tenant gradually to acquire more land as he showed the capability to develop it. In Norfolk and other counties people could not get a little bit of land—in fact in many cases they were denied the right to keep a pig. That was what the Government by the Small Holdings Act were determined to put an end to and to bring the people upon the soil. (Applause.) They hoped to -do -a great deal by legislation but in the meantime much was being accomplished by administra- tion. They had in the President of the Board of 'Agriculture the best landlord in England. (Applause.) He (Mr. Tomkinson) had been to see his estate and he I believed a more noble and generous-hearted landlord

According to Sir Erasmus Wilson, the pores by which the perspiration is carried on average 3.528 to the square inch of skin surface. Krause put the total number over the whole body at 3,381,218. These glands, placed together, would make an orifice twice the size of the human mouth.

Little wonder, therefore, that a diseased skin has a bad effect on the general health.- Little wonder, too, that Zam-Buk, the 'Well-known skin- cure, by cleansing the pores and dispelling disease, is often said to improve bodily health at the same time. For in Zam-Buk it is conceded that we have a balm of rare richness and remark- ably skin-renewing power. It is a preparation which puts one in mind of the many excellent oi!s and balms used by the Grecian ladies of fashion in bygone days, who, if they took pride in anything, certainly bestowed every pain and attention to ensure a soft, smooth, and healthy skin. In recent years the anxiety to produce the beauty of skin that is only "skin deep"—and not always that —has led to the intro- duction of ointments, salves, and embrocations which generally possess one or two grave objections.

I What nonsense, continued the speaker, was being talked ' at the present day! They heard a great deal of SOCIALIST TALK which seemed to say that the Liberal party had had its - day and was not going to do any more for the people! and therefore they must have the reign of Socialism. I She was certain everyone in that hall wanted progress and social reform, and they believed that the Liberal party could and would do it. (Applause.) The beauty of Liberalism was that it never stood still ; it was like a growing child. Perhaps some of the Liberals one had beard of many years ago in the House of Commons, splendid men though they were, would not be what one would call Liberals to-day, becausWthe day of the old- fashioned individualism had gone. They did their work in their day, but now that we bad got the voice and the wishes of the people we had entered upon an era of social reform. (Applause.) It was not "each man for himself and the devil take the hindmost,", but it, was "each for all and all for each." (Applause.) She did not say that there were not here and there in the Liberal party men of the old-fashioned kind, fossils—(laughter)—but if they were there what was the reason ? It was because there were still some fossilised constituencies, and they must not blame the Liberal party for that. The very essence of Liberalism was that it grew with the times and expinded and enlarged its ideas, because the basis of it was PROGRESS AND TRUST OF THE PEOPLE, whereas the Tories—Conservatives they called them- selves now, and she liked the word better because it really expressed what they were—wanted to keep every- thing as it was, good, bad or indifferent. Liberals wanted to keep the good and get rid of what was bad. (Applause.) The greatest Liberal who ever lived— William Ewart Gladstone—(aPplartse)—began life as a Tory, and therefore knew the fories inside' and out,.and it was because he knew them that he became a Liberal. He said that the essence of Liberalism was trust in the people qualified by prudence, and the essence of Toryism was distrust of the people qualified by fear. What was true then was true now, and whatever the Tories call themselves—Tory democrats or Tory Socialists if they liked—they were in their innermost hearts Conservative' of all that was good, bad or indifferent. Liberalism in the past triumphed over Conservatism, and gave the people a chance of education. He had been told that the critics outside were not malicious, although he thought some of the criticisms were rather scathing. The Health Committee and the Town Council generally welcomed fair-minded criticism if the motive was to stir up what was thought to be indifference. If the criticism was in the best interests of the town the Council would welcome it. Councillor BARTON seconded. Alderman BENNETT was delighted to find there was a resolution about the smoke question,. and that the inspector had been taking observations about the emission of dense black smoke, but the committee seemed timid and wanted to get information from other towns to strengthen their hands. They must not be surprised to find that; other towns were as lax as Warrington was, but he hoped that would not prevent the Health Committee putting the law into operation. It was a lamentable fact that many of their municipalities were wanting in public spirit and real intelligence in dealing with this partidular question. He hoped that drastic action would be taken, as the emission of this dense black smoke was a barbarism which ought not to be allowed in any civilized country. (Laughter and applause.) Councillor EVANS said the primary business of the committee was the consideration of the health of the people. They had gone into the smoke question, and he would speak more fully about it later on; but it should be remembered that while smoke might not make a town pleasant there was one town namely Sheffield—known as "the smoky town "—where the death-rate was 16.7. He could not think that the best interests of the town were • served by everlastingly hampering manufacturers about the smoke nuisance. He might inform the Council that in 1902 there werel 2,506 nuisances reported—and among them was the I smoke question—and last year the number was 2,368. The smoke inspector since his appointment had steadily with common-sense and ploddingly done his duty. He had no desire to hamper the manufacturers of the town. (Hear, hear.) CORPORATION TO GROW ITS OWN TREES. The Health Committee resolved :—" That the offer of the Town Hall, Parks and Baths Committee of £5 per annum for about two acres •of land belonging to this committee at Sankey be accepted." A long discussion ensued, and it was pointed out that the Corporation intended to use the land is a nursery and cultivate trees there. They would thus be able to • supply trees which were required for street-planting, &c. A considerable saving would be affected. Ultimately the minutes were approved. TREE-PLANTING. The Town Hall, Parks and Baths Committee resolved that, on the request of the Paving and Sewerage Com- mittee, the land adjoining the Police Station in Museum-street be laid out and planted with shrubs and that tree be planted on the land at the corner of Manchester-road and Padgate-lane. Councillor STEEL said with regard to the planting of trees at the corner of Padgate-lane' workmen had for some time been forming a footpath there, and had laid down the flags. It seemed a pity some arrangement could not be arrived at between the different com- mittees when this sort of thing had to be done. It would save expense, because now some of the flags would have to be taken up to plant the trees. Councillor PEMBERTON said that was the usual way of doing things. Councillor CRAIK said he told the head gardener at the time the minute was passed that the Paving and Sewerage Committee were about to lay the footpath, and he asked him to consult with the Borough Surveyor as to the position in which the trees would be fixed. The minutes were passed on the motion of Alderman BOLTON, seconded by the MAYOR. THE MARKETS. A meeting of the Markets Committee was held an October 7th when,on the suggestion of the Town Clerk, instructions were given to revise the whole of the bye-laws and regulations for the markets. The Committee resolved that not more than four stalls be let to any tenant in future. Councillor TOOLE said the latter resolution would be a hardship to some of the people in the market who were unable to find accommodation for all their goods onlfour stalls. The matter ought to be further con- sidered and he moved that it be referred back. ' There was no seconder, and the minutes were then passed.

ALLEGED SHORTAGE OF WATER.

At a meeting of the Water Committee on October 7th, a letter was read from Messrs R. A. Naylor, Limited, with regard to the alleged insufficiency of water in the mains near their works for fire purposes. Alderman EVANS, moving the adoption of the minutes, gave full particulars of the supply and said the com- mittee considered it was ample. He also believed the Fire Engine Committee were satisfied. Alderman FORSHAW seconded, and the minutes were agreed to. MORE ABOUT STANDARD WAGES.

The Electricity and Tramways Committee decided that the tender of the St. Helens Cable Co. Ltd., for the supply of cables for 12 months aceoxding to specifi- cation and schedule of prices be accepted. Councillor STOTT moved an amendment that the minute be referred back. He had nothing against the firm and did not adopt this attitude in any vindictive snirit. The Council, however' ought to insist on the Standing Order being obeyed and he thought he was able to show conclusively on a previous occasion that the conditions obtaining under the firm in question were not in accord Ince with .the Standing Orders. He alleged that the firm made an attempt to use the registry 'office maintained by the rates to secure workmen at something like 40 per cent less than the standard rate paid in the town for that class of labour. If the Council did not enforce the Standing Order it was 'equal to subsidising the firm which did not pay the standard rate and enabling them to enter into unfair competition with those who did pay it. If the Chair- man had information to show that the St. Helens Cable Company did conform to the rules he would be per- fectly satisfied. Councillor PEMBERTON seeonded. Th 3 MAYOR: This kind of thing is dreadful, I must confess. The. Tows CLERK. said the resolution was passed on

the understanding that the firm paid the standard rate. Before the contract was given, the Borough Treasurer would endeavour to ascertain the rate the firm paid. The MAYOR : If you want to drive the trade out of the country you must just follow the advice of Coun- cillor Stott.

Councillor STorr : I strongly object to that. If you are going to have Standing Orders lam going to have them adhered to, whether trade is driven out of the country or not.

I The MAYOR said if the contract had not been given to this country i would have gone abroad to the 'lowest tenderer; but some members said it would be I better to keep the order in towrO, 1 In answer to Councillor Pemberton, the MAYOR said there were English tenders higher than the Cable , Company's. • I Councillor PEMBERTON : Because they pay the standard rate of wages. ContinuingN.he said if public bodies were to accept the lowest tender onany condition they would he putting a premium on those who , employed cheaper labour. He did not agree in driving trade from the town, but he thouhgt the terms ought to , be equal. i councillor WILKINSON said the Standing Order bad been put before the St. Helens Cable Company, and they said they paid the currant rate of wages in the district. Therefore it was for Councillor Stott to prove that they did not. Councillor PEMBERTON : It is for them to prove that they do. Councillor BALL said it was generally knonn and , admitted that the firm did not pay the standard rate to; ordinary workmen, but they contended that in the work of electric cable making they did pay the standard rate. Their argument was based on the fact that they were the onl3 makers in the district. The Standing Orders, were not sufficiently stringent to enable the Council to pass the firm by. Councillor STurr said that so long as the Committee I would satisfy themselves that the firm paid the standard rate he would agree to them having the contract. The MAYOR: We have given strict orders for the matter to be looked into.

The amendment was withdrawn and the minutes were approved. INCREASE OF SALARY.

DEPUTATION EXPENSES.

At a meeting of the General Purposes Committee, the Borough Treasurer submitted a return of expenses paid to members of the Council for serving on deputations during the three years ended 31st May, 1907, in accord- ance with the resolution of the Council of the 4th June, 1907. ,

Counciller STOTT as the member who asked for the return to be submitted, said that considering the figures covered a period of three years they were very moderate indeed, except in one or two instances which might be termed rather high. The chief object he had in view was to secure a report from the members comprising deputations and to see that the deputations should not be confined to a small circle but that every Councillor should have a fair share of that kind of work Councillor CHANTLER th7nght the return ought to be published. The I.lfAvoß said it was issued as a private circular. Councillor STOTT said his resolution was simply for the return to be produced to the Council. The minutes were approved.

Warrington Cattleman's Death.

The death of a cattleman named John Oakes, 38 years of age, who had resided at Crossley-street, War- rington, was inquired into-at Birkenhead on Saturday. Deceased had been a cattleman on board the steam- ship Turcoman, which left Montreal for Liverpool on the 12th inst. On the 21st he took ill, and subse- quently grew worse. When the steamer was off the Isle of Man on Friday morning he succumbed, and his body was conveyed to the Birkenhead Mortuary, where a post-martem examination, was held. Dr. Preece, police-surgeon, said he found pneumonia present in the lungs, the cause of death being hemorrhage of the lungs and stomach, consequent upon pneumonia and cirrhosis of the liver. The condition of the latter was due to over-indulgence in alcohol. A. verdict in accordance with the medical evidence was returned.

HUSBAND AND WIFE CURED.

Chas. FORBES Bile Beans End Indigestion, Bilious Headaches Chest and Back Pains,

and Weakness after Influenza.

From a state of chronic suffering to a life of health and happiness" describes the great change ems. FORDE'S bile beans made in Mrs. Louise Littlechild, of 111, Roman road, South Shields. "For many years I was a sufferer from indiges- tion, flatulence, and other digestive ailments," Mrs. Littlechild told a South Shields reporter. "I had severe wind spasms and experienced great difficulty in getting my breath. I suffered from sharp pains in the chest and back, sleepless nights, and in the mornings I had fits of dizziness. "These were followed by bilious headaches, so had that I was unable to lift my head from the pillow,and I was obliged to remain in bed generally until tea-time, unable to eat a bite or drink any- thing. I had to give up eating almost all solid food, as nothing would digest. At different times I consulted several doctors, who gave me relief for a few days. Then the attacks would com- mence again, worse than ever. I also tried several patent medicines but with equally unsatisfactory results, until I was rersuaded to try Cius. FORDE'S bile beans.

Beer is said to be a Stilllll THE ELECTIONS. Wit. The results of yestee day's two election contests in Warrington were decided in favour of—and perhaps by—beer. If this does not stimulate the temperance cause, and put backbone into the Nonconformists then Warrington is indeed given over to the enemy ! We are saddened at the want of perception shown by the Warrington electorate in these contests ; their manliness must have become a dull and spiritless thing if they can be won over 30 simply by the representatives of the trade which openly aims at securing the domination of the municipality of Warringtou. But while we regret the issue of the fight we are not in the least despondent. The gauntlet has been thrown down, and we have faith lin the latent spirit of the people of Warrington to pick it up and wage war with a singleness of purpose which has been manifested before. Tile Light is only just beginning and we can find heart to rejoice that, at any rate, the issue is not obscured. The drink demon must be fought and strangled befofe it gets a resistless hold on the town. Toe victory of the brewers' representa- tives yesterday was complete, but we foresee an early reversal of this hasty verdict. Now that the call to arms has been ratseel we trust that all the progressive forces will unite to rehabilitate Warrington's fair fame !

The Town Council has A CORPORATIoN. decided to have a nursery— NURSERY. and any chance visitor to the Council meeting on Tuesday might very naturally have concluded that he had,by mistake, got into that nursery, for the character of some of the discussions bordered on the childish. But the nursery in question, for which the Town Hall Parks and Baths Committee is paying the Health Committee £5 a year, consists of two acres of land at Sankey which it is intended to use for the cultivation of trees and shrubs destined for the public streets and parks. Alderman Bolton seems to have been absent from the Coin mittee meeting at which the nursery ven- ture waa decided upon, and apparently for this reason chiefly he opposed the scheme in Council. We know no one who can be more cantankerous than the Deputy Mayor when he wants, and he wanted badly on Tuesday. After making all sorts of frivolous objections he finally withdrew his amendment. They would be "landed into a white elephant"he said amid much laughter. We remember when the municipal bowling green question was first mooted Alderman Bolton was a bitter opponent and said that "grass would not grow in Warrington." Apparently there is less difficulty in growing grass and shrubs than orchids and even Alderman Bolton can grow orchids in Warrington. We hope the nursery will be a success. It ought to have the paternal blessing of the Beautiful Warrington Society. -

To use his own words, the MR. CROFIELD, Member for Warrington has M.P. "ON THE been "on the stump " Srumr." during the past week. Commencing with Friday evening he spoke at a meeting of the Junior Liberals, and at a social gathering and prize dis tribution at the Liberal Club. His speech to the youngest branch of the Liberal party was at once the longest and the most comprehensive he has ever delivered in Warrington. It covered almost the whole range of contemporary politics and was delivered with an ease and logical force which maintained the keen interest of the audience from start to finish. Mr. Crosfield made a lengthy criticism of Socialism and the Communistic idea which should exercise the minds of those burning enthusiasts whose plan for social reform includes the revolution of the existing order of things in this comparatively well-ordered State. .From politics, the Member passed on to sport at the Liberal Club, and again on Saturday at Manchester he regaled the members of the Lancashire and Cheshire Bowling Association (of which he is President) with one of those chatty, reminiscent speeches which come so well from the lips of such a pronounced out- door man. On Monday evening Mr. Crosfleld was seen in the political field once more, as chairman of the joint demonstration organized by the Women's Liberal Association and the Progressive Labour League. Another address of some length was welcomed, for Mr. CROSFIELD can suit himself to his hearers, and on this occasion he had much to say of interest to the large number of ladies present. Woman's sphere in politics as well as municipal government is broadening rapidly, and the Member for Warrington welcomes the fact with the earnestness of a true progressive. He has had a busy week-end, but we are sure be has enjoyed the opportunity of meeting so many supporters and friends in the realms of both politics and sport. The next events to look forward to in local politics are the visit of the Right Hon. AUGUSTINB BIRRELL (Chief Secretary for Ireland) in December, and Mr. CROSFIELD'S second annual account of his stewardship of the electoral Borough of Warrington early in the new year. In fact the breeze of Liberal ismis blowing with such steadiness and vigour that there will be no difficulty in keeping the flag flying in Warrington for a long time to come!

The capital invested in THE HORRORS OF English railways stood last A &BIKE. year at 1,287 millions; the receipts 117 f millions ; the passengers 1,240 A ; the goods and minerals conveyed 459 million tons. If a striks involved a quarter of the traffic, its consequence would be disastrous and unthinkable. The central committee of the syndicate of railway servatits in Italy have pronounced against a strike,satisfied to know "that it needed only one word from itself to arrest the life of the nation." The last clause describes the situation. With 30 per cent added to the cost of living, the loss to the community, whose average expenditure is 15s. per family, would stand at two millions sterling per week. Undoubtedly the cost of necessities would be increased. The companies employ 391,681 men; the Amalgamated and allied societies command 143,000. The weekly revenue of the companies is over two millions. Directors might bargain on 1 losing 25 per cent. of that and on expenaes going! up simultaneously. At present the average dividend is less than 3i ! But the weekly loss of over half-I a million to the shareholders, and over a quarter of a million to the men, are merely incithnts in a grim nightmare stcry which ()illy the imagination of a romantic novelist like Mr. H. G. Weirs could properly set before us.

Sale of Work.

The annual sale af work in connection with St. Peter's Church was held in the schools, John-street, on Monday and Tuesday. There was an attractive display of useful and fancy articles, the stalls being presided over by the following :—Vicarage stall : Mrs. Percival, Mrs. Greaves, Mrs. Heacock, Mrs. East, and Miss Young. Refreshment stall : Mrs. Bamforth, Mrs. Webster, Mrs. Lingley, Mrs. Yeomans, Mrs. Skelland, and Miss Barlow. Men's stall: Mrs. Steel, Mrs. Russell, Mrs. Rylance. Aivertisement stall : Mrs. Cowdell, Mrs. Joseph Wilkinson and the Misses Wilkinson. Toy stall : Misses Lily Percival, Horton, Dennett, Forshaw, and A. Kenyon. Flower stall: Misses Stanley, Webster, and Sarah Young. Sweet still: Misses Percival, Hughes, and M. Boyle.—The sale was opened on Monday by the Mayoress (Mrs. Smethurst.) Councillor Fulton Smith, who presided, said they were now passing through a serious crisis in social matters, but they hoped that good would pre- vail aud that the evil genius of Socialism as it was now expounded to them would be swept out of the country because should it have the upper hand he feared it would be a sad day for dear old England. (Applause.) —The Rev. B. Percival congratulated Councillor Fulton-Smith on having had a " walkover" with regard to the representation of that ward in the Town Council, and said he hoped he would live long to represent them.—On Tuesday the sale was re- opened by Mrs. John Fairclough. The total proceeds of the sale amounted to £B6.

THE EXAMINER,

NOVHAIBBR 2nd, 1907.

It is a pertinent question WHAT IS A which Lord ROBERT CECIL CONSERVATIVE? asks in a letter to "The

Times " regarding the qualification for inclusion in the Conservative Party, and it is more particularly interesting because of the recent attempts on the part of some good' people, mostly Tariff Reformers, to scare us out of our wits by shouting, "Socialism ' in our ears. Lord ROBERT is not a Tariff Reformer, but his Conservatism is unquestionable. For all that, there is not much doubt that he will be drummed out of the party before the next election simply because he is a Free Trader, and declines to abandon the traditional policy of his party as laid down by successive Conservative leaders for many years past. liefeels that he has some right to complain, when he finds one of the most prominent of Tariff Reformers using a Conservative platform to advocate the nationalisation of railways and canals payment of members, transference of the cost of education from the rates to the taxes, adult male and female suffrage, taxation of land values, non-contributory old-age pensions of ss. a week and contributory pensions in addi- tion. State-feeding and clothing of school- children, and many other such items of policy. These surely are hardly distinguishable from the programme of the Labour members in the House of Commons, and yet because that left wing of the party of progress has advocated some of these the whole organisation of the Conservative Party fs to be turned out to fight "Socialism." Lord ROBERT CECIL is quite frank. He does not like this programme, and he does not think it orthodox Conservatism. But the person who advocates it is allowed without question to remain a member of the party because he has the Tariff Reform hook hidden under this bait ; while Lord ROBERT, whose Conservatism has no tinge of this Socialistic colouring, is to be driven from Parliament because he believes in cheap food for the people. There is no question that if the present Opposition had to decide between Lord ROBERT CECIL and MT. WATSON RUTHERFORD, M.P., whose programme he criticises, they would keep Mr. RUTHERFORD because of his Fiscal Policy, because he utters the shibboleth about making the foreigner pay, and because he is pre- pared to tax the necessaries of life. We do not complain. The Consarvative Party has the right to make what conditions it likes for member- ship of the party, and the only condition at present seems to be a belief in a Tariff Reform. But we have some right of complaint when these same Tariff Reformers try to get the people on their side by advocating some of the very measures which have led them to enter on the campaign against Socialism. The new programme advocates the taxation of land values. It is hardly two months since the House of Lords threw out the Scottish Valuatiou Bill because it was a first step towards the separate taxation of land, and we have some recollection that the Opposition in the House of Commons fought the measure. Tariff Reformers are insincere when they are attacking Liberals for being Socialists, and Lord ROBERT CECIL obviously knows it. He ask now if belief in Tariff Reform is the only qualification for a" Conservative " Member of Parliament. It would seem so, for some of the reforms now being advocated are certainly such as would never have been dreamt of five years ago. Promise anything

NOTES AND NOTIONS. .1

In the course of a speech on Tuesday, Alderman Bennett said "the Aluseum Committee deals with things which are eternal, and not with those which are transitory." Which category do the field-mice and snakes come under we wonder !

Mr. R. Pierpoint advanced a sound reason on Thursday for the retention of the House of Lords. The second chamber was necessary he said, "to make grammatical some of the erratic sentences sent up from the House of Commons." 0 _

Mr. J. Howes, with alai facility for hitting the nail on the head which distinguishes him, referred at Monday evening's demonstration in Parr Hall to the fact that at the General Election the Liberal Party "fought for cheap food and meant to continue the fight." The present high price of bread was due, he explained, to the shortage of wheat all over the world. But under Free Trade bread was cheaper, and would remain cheaper in this country than in any other country in the world.

* * * We have to heartily congratulate Mr. J. T. Locker on his unopposed election to the Town Council. Councillor Locker will make a valuable addition to the Liberal ranks. The members of the Association are particularly pleased that their " hon. sec." should have thus been honoured by his townsmen. Coun- cillors Hawthorn, Skelland, Wilkinson, Burton and Ellison also have our congratulations on their return without a contest. 0 .4

His Worship the Mayor was quite overcome by the chorus of praise which sounded in his ear on Tuesday evening when the Council formally thanked him for the " satsfactory manner in which he has discharged the responsible duties." Members vied with one another in heaping encomiums on his Worship's silvery head, and we should think he never felt quite such a paragon of virtue. The compliments were all genuine, and most of them well-deserved. Alderman Smethurst has made a capital Mayor—hard-working, impartial, and courteous—and he merits the thanks of the community.

* * * Election ardour on the part of Mr. W. Rogers or one of his friends has led to an indiscretion which should not pass unnoticed by the Beautiful Warring- ton Society. Nearly every blank wall and gable end in Town Hall Ward is defaced by the huge inscription in chalk "Rogers X." It struck us as being some- what incomplete. "Rogers' XX X " would surely have been better.

0 -zr,

Some of the Tory election posters are distinctly funny. For instanca Mr. W. B. Roberts said to the people of Howley: " You want to save a bit. Vote for Roberts and better wages." We fail to see the connection. How is Mr. W. B. Roberts going to improve the wages of the workers ? The trade he belongs to has more to do with creating poverty and keeping wages low than any other. If he really desires people to "save a bit" he should advise them to sign the pledge. Then they would save a lot ! * * *

Another true blue poster advises the electors to vote for "Rogers, Roberts, and religious equality," and promises clear, distinct, and solid religious instruc- tion" by "teachers who believe in the instruction they give." The poster then offers £lOO if it can be proved that the Radical party supports this policy. Somebody ought to claim that £lOO at once, if they know where to get it from. The Radical policy does include religious instruction, but not denominational instruction in the State schools. When it suits them our Tory friends can apparently gloss over the differ- ence. What they really want is not religious instruc- tion, but instruction in their particular dogma pro- vided at the public expense. The Radical policy is that the sects must provide their own instruction in the faith they profess. * *

The " Electrical Times " in its review of the accounts of the Warrington Electric Supply under- taking,passes a complimentary opinion on the manage- ment of the local department which should be gratify- ing to the Warrington Electricity Committee and Engineer, reads as follows :—" A considerable decrease in the average price obtained from the three classes of supply is indicated, the total having fallen to 10. per unit. Yet on so low a rate of charge sub- stantial profits have been earned, the balance to the good on the year's work exceeding £2,500. Such a yield would obviously have been impossible but for the high standard of economical working that had been established."

It was not ever thus but when they do agree their unanimity is wonderful "Morning Leader." • • At least a dozen women in different parts of the kingdom are now seeking seats upon the borough councils, opened to them this year by the passing of the Qualification of Women (Borough and County Councils) Act. Manchester, Bolton, Brecon, Burslem, Croydon, Edinburgh, Eastbourne, Hull, High Wycombe, Leicester, Oxford, and Swansea have each one candidate. In addition a medical woman will be put forward by the Labour Representation Committee for a vacancy that will shortly arise on the Newcastle City Council. Southport, which early elected an unusual number of women to the Board of Guardians, had at one time the prospect of a woman candidate for the Town Council, but a little while ago Mrs. Rimmer announced her withdrawal because the Liberals had not left her a seat to contest. In almost every case the woman candidate has already proved herself in public work. * *

Coroner Brighonse has been running" amok " just recently, and we cannot see that he has added much that is enviable to his reputation in the "little breezes" at Warrington and Widnes. The other day at a Warrington inquest the Coroner,in his high mightiness chose to condemn the new Chief Constable in that 'official's absence. Said Mr. Brighouse, "If this is a sample of the new regime, I prefer the on," Since then it has transpired that the Chief Constable was absent for an excellent reason, and that be was not to blame for any peculiar circumstances which aroused the Coroner's ire. The Town Council has expressed complete confidence in their officer,and the magistrates on Tuesday went so far as to suggest that the Coroner owed Mr. Nicholls an apology. We hope the Coroner will he generous and admit thafi ) was unjust to an absent defendant in his own Court. The other little matter rests between Mr. Brighouse and Mr. J. W. Knowles. Both are solicitors and both have an equally good opinion of themselves. No apology seems to be due eitber way. * * _

If Councillor Wilkinson read the " Evening Chronicle" on Thursday evening, he must have rubbed his eyes hard and wondered whether he was dreaming, for under the heading " War- rington Sportsman Candidate," the journal announced tint "Mr. W. M'Neill Sloane, is making a strenuous effort against Councillor Wilkinson in Orford Ward at Warrington. Mr. Sloane was the originator of the first Association football club in the Cheshire capital, the Chester Wanderers, which was formed in the early eighties, and was for several seasons a eentre forward in the field. He is also an old racing cyclist and athlete, having won many one mile handicaps. He is the president of the Dublin Conservative Association." It is conceivable that Mr. Sloane may also have been a little bit staggered at this circum- stantial piece of "news." The facts are, of course, that Councillor Wilkinson has been returned unopposed and Mr. Sloane is not a candidate, though a nomination paper was put in on his behalf against Councillor Skelland, and then withdrawn. We wonder if the " Chronicle " has the gift of second sight and relates what may be happening three years hence!
