THE TORY MEETING.

CRITICAL NOTES ON THE SPEECHES.

rtiM w

\ ' w ,

The Tory ticket meeting has come and gone. There was a large attendance but little enthusiasm. Evidently the flood of Tory oratory did not stimulate the audience to any great extent. The Right Hon. A. Lyttelton devoted almost the whole of his criticism to the Socialistic propaganda. Perhaps the most amusing part of his speech was his reference to the late Lord Beaconsfield as the great social reformer. " No- one painted with more vividness the sufferings of the poor "said Mr. Lyttleton, and we might add that no- one more persistently ignored their sufferings or refused to satisfy their aspirations. It would have been instructive if Mr. Lyttelton had endeavoured to point to some of the wise measures carried either by Beaconsfield, Salisbury, Balfour or Chamberlain for the improvement of the condition of the poor. The late Government, we were told, appointed a Royal Commission to study the effect of the Poor Law with a view to an experiment in the direction of old age pensions. Tory Governments are noted for Royal Commissions and inquiries, but unfortunately they are never able to get beyond that. The problems that the late Government inquired into the present Government has been stesdily solving, and if the dead weight of Toryism in the Second Chamber can be overcome, reforms will be carried much more speedily than they have been in the past. Mr. LYTTELTQN evidently felt that he was treading on tender ice when he came to deal with the question of Protection, mis-named Tariff Reform. He spoke of " the desire of the Unionist party (with some dissen- tients, with whom they must reason) to adjust the Customs of this country in such a manner as to bring the foreigner into partnership with us in some of the burdens we had to bear." Seeing that under our present system we have a larger foreign trade in pro- portion to our population than any other country,'n the world, seeing also that we are the largest Our builders and exporters, and that all these advantages have come to us since we abolished the old Tory methods of Protection and opened our ports to the world, it would have been interesting if the hon. gentleman had pointed out how this partner ship, which he suggests, will increase our wealth or lighten our burdens. In reference to these matters Mr. Lyttelton was wise in avoiding details and dealing only in generalities. If his party would tell us what articles they propose to tax and what is to be the j amount of taxation there would be very few people, especially those connected with our commercial arrangements, who would dare to render them much assistance at the time of a general election. Mr. W. PETER RYLANDS told us that the principle underlying the Conservative party is "the principle which aims in the broadest sense at the best for the nation and the empire, and for the individual fair play all round." We wonder whether this sentence was uttered in ignorance or with intent to deceive. In view of the fact that every great reform on the Statute Book has been resisted by the Tory PartY until resistance was no longer possible ; remember- ing that they opposed the opening of our ports and the giving of cheap food to the people, that they offered determined opposition to the extension of the franchise, the passing of the Ballot Act, the removal of religious disabilities, Catholic emancipa- tion and to all the great measures of reform which have made the past hundred years so glorious, vre feel that Mr. Rylands must be a man of considerable courage to make the above declaration at the present time when we have just finished a melancholy and distressing period of Tory rule during which the old horrors of Toryism were repeated with savage enthusiasm. Mr. RYLANDS then claimed for his party the fulfil' ment of promises, evidently forgetting the many times that his friend Mr. Chamberlain and others have recommended old age pensions, and the distirct promise given in Birmingham re compensation for injuries to workmen, when we were told that they (the Conservatives) desired to give "compensation for ever/ accident." A long catalogue of promises made by Tory statesmen and not yet fulfilled could he here recorded if space permitted. Dealing with Colonial matters Mr. Rylands out-Heroded Herod. He said that "under the administration of Mr. Chamberlain, ably succeeded by Mr. Lyttelton, there has been a, development in the Colonial and imperial idea sun and an appreciation of what colonies really mean such as never existed throughout the whole hi storY of our empire." Probably Mr. Rylands was not referring to South Africa. There the Colonial policy of Mr. Chamber- lain and Mr. Lyttelton destroyed two republics, abolished peace, brought about a long and costly war, closed the labour market to the British workingman and flooded it with alien Chinese, and this state a confusion, disorder and degradation had to be grappled with by the present Government, which has already succeeded in restoring the right of self- government, establishing a friendship between British and Dutch, and clearing out the Chinese at a rapid rate in order to open out South Africa as a field for British labour. It is amazing that Tory speakers can so trade upon the supposed short memories of the people as to make such statements as those deliversd by Mr. Rylands so soon after the sad exhibition a colonial blunders perpetrated by the late Tory Govern raent. The remainder of Mr. Rylands' speech was more amusing than logical. He treated his audience to ten minutes fire works, and although his remarks did not contain much instruction, we hope they Pro- vided a little entertainment.

IZCZ;:r-13-1-E-i-tr—o-t-s—T -undertook the defence of the House of Lords, confusing the question of the heredi- tary obstacle against which Liberals contend with the necessity for a Second Chamber. Of course all reformers recognize that it is possible to have a Second Chamber without having an hereditary House 01, Lords, and it is because the Liberals desire the will 01 the people to prevail that our Prime Minister apPe3l9 to the nation for assistance to enable him flt make the people's decision supreme. Ur. Pierpoint's speech was most important for what it did not contain. Scale time ago our Member, Mr. Crosfield, wrote calling Mr. Pierpoint's attention to the question of the Chinese problem a South Africa and criticising the doings of our so-called 'Unionist friends in regard to that matter. The letter contained a distinct challenge to Mr. Pierpoint concerning a few of the most important events with reference to the Tory responsibility for the flooding of the labour market of South Africa viit.ll alien Chinese. Mr. Pierpoint altogether ignored this question and Mr. Crosfield's letter, and in so doing be certainly showed an amount of wisdom which does Ilyn credit. We take it that the people will not readily forget the terrible responsibility attaching to the TorY party in reference to this dark page of Colonial history, and although Tory speakers may avoid the issusrer memory of these things will still remain in the public mind and the altered condition of things in South Africa,brought about by the present Liberal administra- tion, will have its influence when the people are again invited to go to the polls.

1 An Ironworker's Death. Mr. Coroner Brighouse held an inquest on Tuesday' ' afternoon, on the body of Frederick Monks, aged 36 years, an ironworker's labourer, who had resided at 23, Winifred-street, Warrington. Mr. Suniner,factory inspector, attended. Mr. -F. G. Taylor appeared on behalf of Messrs. Pearson and Knowles, and Mr. Steel for the relatives of the deceased. The Coroner said the deceased had been employed as a labourer at Messrs. Pearson and Knowles' Dallain Forge. On the third of June he met with an accident at his work," his foot was crushed across the instep. He W5B removed to the Infirmary, where he remained for a month. Then he came out and walked on crutches, and was treated as an out-patient. Lately he had bees declining in health, and he died on Friday the 25th inst., before the arrival of the doctor who was suni- moned.—Dr. Wright said the cause of death wag failure of the heart's action, with commencing acute congestion of the lungs. He found the foot la ! healthy conditon.—The jury returned a verdict of ‘` Death from natural causes," and expreesed their sympathy with the relatives.—Mr. Taylor also added the company's sympathy, seeing that Monks had rot with an accident in their works.—Mr. Steel said Mr Monks was well known in the town. The relatives were quite satisfied with the result of the pot' mortem examination.

Cheshire Hunt Mishaps. Some bad luck attended the hunt with the Cheshire Hounds on Tuesday from Church Minshull. Mr. James Tomkinson, M.P., lost a valuable hunter, which was injured by a stake in a fence and was shot, and a hunter ridden by Miss Court, of Middlewich, was torn by barbed wire. Police Inspector's Retirement. Inspector Alfred James Taylor having given notice of his desire to retire from the police force after upwards of 26 years' service, and the Chief Constable having certified the service as approved service for the purpose of the Police Acts, the Watch Committee have resolved that there be paid out of the police pension fund to Mr. Taylor, a pension for life equal to two-thirds of his annual pay.

Mr. J. Maginn's Annual Sale. As will be seen from an advertisement in another column, Mr. John Maginn will hold his annual sixteen days' clearance sale by auction at the Royal Court Sale Rooms, Rylands-street, commencing on Wednes- day next. A. very high-class stock of furniture is offered, the value exceeding £6,100. The whole of the stock will be on view on Monday and Tuesday next.

Y. Branch B.W.T.A. A meeting of the above Branch was held at Silver-street Schools on Wednesday. Mrs. Moore - spoke to the Y. members and members of the Girls Club on the object of their programme for the coming season. There were nearly GO present, solos were given by the Misses Cox, Blackstock and Jones. Recitations by Miss Taylor.

Property Sale. Mr. Alfred H. Clive conducted a property sale at the Blue Bell Inn, Horsemarket-street, on Wednesday evening, Lot 1 conststing of seven dwellinghouses and premises, Nos. 136 to 148, Ellesmere-street, were purchased by Mr. John 4. Gittens for a client, for the sum of £605. Lot 2, six dwelling-houses and premises 67 to 77, Foster-street, off Orford-lane was also knccked down to Mr. J. H. Cittens for a client, the price being £6Ol. Lot 3, consisting of two dwelling- houses and premises 5 and 7, Naylor-street, were bought by Mr. Samuel Gough for £l6O. Mr. Charles H. Unsworth was the solicitor for the vendors.

Independent Order of Oddfellows. A dance and social in connection with the Admiral Nelson Lodge of the Independent Order of Oddfellows took place at the Co-operative Hall, Sankey-street, on Thursday evening. There was a large attendance. Bro Webster (who presided) said the members derived greater benefit from their lodge than they did in any other lodge in the town. (Applause.) During the evening songs were rendered by Misses Fairhurst, Plinston, Woodland, and Messrs. Lot Brooks and Reid;, Humorous selections were given by Mr. E. A. Sanders.

Motor Cyclists Summoned. At the St. Helens Police Court, yesterday, Alfred Skinner, Hill-top, Latchford, Warrington, and Harvey Orr Adams, 1, l3ewsey-road, Warrington, pleaded guilty to driving motor-cycles at a speed dangerous to the public. P.C. Anders said defendants were travelling at fully 1S miles an hour. Defendants denied that the speed was so great, and said they had full control of their machines. The Chi Constable said from inquiries he had made he found that the defendants were very respectable men and careful drivers, so far as was known. What he had heard was all in their favour. The Bench ordered defendants to pay 11s, costs each.

Palace and Hippodrome. An excellent entertainment is provided at the Palace this week. The bill is headed by Alexandra. Dagmar, the well-known comedienne and raconteur, who delights the audience with her songs and stories. A highly amusing turn is that of Tom Fay and Com- pany in the absutdity "The Yorkshire Lad in London." Ajax and George are very clever gymnasts, and the Gylrnore trio giye a capital pantomimic comedy act. The Sisters Cora are splendid duettists and dancers, and a pleasing feature is Mostol's refined musical act. George Bendon, the white coon, who is a favourite with Warrington audiences, scores a great success in his popular songs. The animated pictures are up-to-date and interesting. PUBLIC BENEFIT.—IINDER THE CLOCK TOR BEST BOOTS IN LANCASHIRE.—At. British Women's Temperance Association. A meeting of the Warrington branch of this associa- tion was held in the Council Chamber at the Town Hall on Thursday afternoon. The Mayoress (Mrs- Smethurst) presided over a good attendance. An address was given by Miss Boyd of Carlisle in which she stated that over £100,000,000 per annum was spent on drink. They wanted counter attractions to the public house such as real temperance hotels etc. 376 babies died in England each year whilst more than half of these died from preventable causes, and a quarter of the deaths were of children under twelve months old. Out of 100 defective children in a school 42 of their parents were addicted to drink. A hearty vote of thanks was accorded to Miss Boyd for her visit and address. During the afternoon Madame Beckett sang several solos whilst Mrs. Jackson Holmes , accompanied.

The sale was re-opened yesterday afternoon by Mrs. AYLMER FROST, of Grappenhall Hall. The Ricroß after again explaining the objects of the bazaar said the subject of Socialism was prominently before them at the present time, but what he had seen and Leard of it he could not say that he very much liked. It seemed to him to be an attempt to get rich in a hurry and he was not at all sure that those who got rich all in a moment would spend their money as wisely and well as those who had made it through a life-time or inherited it. They had all reason in this district to thank those who owned great wealth and property. They had always, he thought, realized their responsibility and had given most generously and nobly. Therefore he was not at alrin the mind to upset things but to leave things as they were and let people gradually rise in the proper way and thdn he hoped they would realise their responsibilities when they had their privileges. Mrs. FROST briefly declared the sale open. Mr. G. BROWN moved a vote of thanks to Mrs. Frost. He remarked that the school in which they were assembled was built by charitable offerings but now ' they had to pay the Education Authority for the use of ' it. When they had got their new Institute he hoped they would be free from Acts of Parliament and have a large hall in which they could hold their parochial . sales, &c. , Mr. F. M. DA.RBY seconded and said a men's institute was an absolute necessity in every parish. The vcte was heattily accorded and Mr. FROST responded. ENTERTAINMENTS.

BOLD-STREET WESLEYAN CHURCH.

Bible Class Social.

The annual social in connection with Bold-street Wesleyan Bible Class was held in the school-room on Thursday evening. The attendance numbered about a hundred, and included Mr. J. C. Holland, who presided, and the Rev. Thomas Miller. The CHAIRMAN said it was his duty as well as privi- lege to extend a very hearty welcome not only to the members of the Bold-street Wesleyan Bible Class but to those friends who had so kindly favoured them with their presence that evening. He would especially empha- sise that with regard to the presence of their esteemed minister for the first time on that occasion. (Applause.) He paid a tribute to the energetic hon. secretary, Mr. J. Leach, for the way in which he had taken charge of the arrangements. The Bold-street Men's Bible class he said had a unique and somewhat interesting history. The school commenced more than 100 years ago in Friars' Green, in what is now a work- shop. There was every probability that the young men's class was originated about the same time but was only traceable for about sixty years. Mr. J. H. Parkinson was teacher for that period. On Mr. Parkinson resigning, in order to become superintentdent (an office which he held for about 25 years) Mr. Henry Bayley, the manager of a large chemical works in Warrington, took up the work. The class at that time was in a very flourishing condition, holding its meetings both morning and afternoon. It had then an attendance of close upon 20 in the morn- ing, and the room was quite full in the afternoon. Mr Bayley was succeeded by Mr. Whitehead, a draughts- man at Bewsey Forge, and a very able man, and thee came Mr. Councillor Dixon, to be followed in a short time by Mr. W. C. Nicholson. It was about the year 1815 when the class migrated to the historic Buttermarket-street school, subsequently to its present premises and he had never kno*n them to have more commodious premises than those which they were in that evening. (Applause.) He had received letters from members who lived in Canada, West Coast of Africa, Calcutta and London to say how they had been helped by a Bible Class, which put him in mind of a well-known verse by John Kahle. The Rev. THOMAS MILLER gave, some sound advice to the young folks and also said that people should read the Bible with more reverence and that it ahould be classed with the best literature. Mr. WILLIAM SILCOCK said he remembered many pleasant associations with the Bible Class and recog- nised the powerful work it was doing. A letter of apology was received from Mr. Reginald Davey, of High Wycombe, Bucks., in which be stated that he had the keenest recollections of the happy times spent at Bold-street and the incalculable benefits derived from the weekly intercourse with members of the class. He wished to be remembered to the Bible class at Warrington, and hoped they would have a very enjoy- able evening. (Applause.) A vote of thanks was accorded to the ladies on the motion of Mr. R. H. SUTTON, treasurer of the class, seconded by Mr. A. M. D UNN, for the w.ay in which the arrangements had been carried out. During the evening solos were rendered by Miss Elsie Taylor and Miss Emily Taylor; a recitation was given by Miss Ethel Cooper, aud selections by the Bold- street Quartette Party, which consisted of Messrs. Warburton and Clayton, Mrs. Warburton and Miss Wood.

THE ELECTIONS.

A Brewers' Victory.

Although there were only two contests for muni- cipal honours at Warrington yesterday, very great interest was taken in them. In both Town Hall and Howley Wards "the trade" made a strenuous effort to increase its representation, the return of the retiring representatives being challenged by Conservatives who are identified with the drill interest. Mr. G-. T. Wilding (N.-T.). was opposed in Town Hall Ward by Mr. W. Rogers, an ex-member of the Council, and a publican, while Mr. W. B. Roberts (head brewer for Greenall's), contested Howley Ward against Mr. W. Clegg (L.) Naturally this aggressiveness on the part of" the trade" was warmly resisted by temperance workers, and appeals to the electors were issued by the Independent Order of Good Templars and the Charity Tent of the Order of Rechabites who strongly deprecated the strengthening of the position of "the trade "in the local Parliament. In Town Hall Ward the non-trading question was again an important issue. Both contests were fought with great keenness and there was a profusion of literature. Yesterday the workers had an exceedingly busy time and a large number of vehicles were brought into requisition to convey the voters to the poll. The booths were situated as follows : TOWN HALL WARD: Infants' School, Kendrick- street. .

HCIVLEY WARD : National Schools, Church-street. THE RESULTS.

At the close of the poll the ballot boxes were con- veyed to the Town Hall, where the counting was expeditiously proceeded with in the Council Chamber The results were announced as follows :

TOWN H kLL WARD. W. Rogers (C.) G. T. Wilding (N.T.)

452 316

Majority HOWLEY WARD. W. B. Roberts (C.) W. Clegg (L.)

Majority

The usual votes of thanks were accorded to the returning officers —The Mayor (Town Hall Ward) and Alderman Evans (Howley Ward).

DECLARING THE RESULT.

A large crowd assembled in front of the Town Hall to hear the declaration of the po d. Councillor W. RoGERs thanke the electors of Town Hall Ward and promised to do his duty in the Council. _

Mr. G. T. Wilding said he could not go away with- out thanking the 316 burgesses who had had the courage to vote against beer. (Cheers.) Without doubt he had done his dutyin the Council and he was willing to continue the work, but beer and other influences had:thrown him out, so be must leave it. Councillor W. B. Roberts did not appear on the balcony to return thanks. Mr. W. CLEGG thanked those who had worked for him and done their best on his behalf. He might say that false issues had been placed before the electors of the ward. (Cries of " Yes" and "No.") Time alone would show who had been the true servant of the ward. He had done his best in times past and he now handed over the reins of office to their friend and representative Mr. W. B. Roberts. He hoped and expected that Councillor Roberts would do his duty to the electors of the Ward and he (Mr. Clegg) would be quite satisfied. (Cheers.)

MR. DODDS AND THE LIQOUR INTEREST.

Strong Appeal to the Electors. rhe following address has been circulated among the electors of Town Hall Ward by Mr. James Dodds. Bewsey Road, October 30th, 1907.

Dear Sir or Madam, I am so deeply impressed by the seriousness of the issue on which you will be called upon to vote on Friday, that I have decided to write to you and to every elector in your important Ward. You have so steadfastly stood by the cause of civic purity since T had the honour to unfurl its flag in your Ward in 1902,, And you have since so consistently elected its supporters, that I am sanguine you will, on this occasion, return its representative—your retiring member Mr. G. T. Wilding—by such a majority as will convince his publican opponent, and all concerned, that you are more determined than ever to stand firmly and solely by the men who have no axes to grind, no personal ends to serve, and who will accept no alder- manic seat, nor make any, corrupt compact with either of the political parties. Yours is no small responsibility. Of late years Warrington has earned wide-spread ill-fame through the corruption which has crept in among many of the members of the Council,l who have allowed their private interests and personal profits to override their desire for the welfare of the whole body of the burgesses. In our strenuous struggle against that appalling condition of our local public affairs, we have never hitherto asked what were a candidate's politics, religion or occupation; if he was competent, honest and fearless we have been content. whether he sold beef, bread, or beer, so long as he WAS against the immoral trading then rampant in the Council chamber, he has had our unqualified support. After great and costly efforts to overcome this deplorable evil tendency, and after having, as we hope and believe, finally eliminated it from the pro- ceedings of the Council, we are confronted with a new dangr, which consists of the attempt of the liquor trade to secure a large share of the Council representation. We see the trade insidiously seeking to get its tied-house tenants, and 'its officials into the Council, for reasons so obvious that I need not detail them. Four men identified with the liquor traffic are now members of the Council, and for the contests to be fought out on Friday, the attacking candidate in each case is a representative of the drink interest. Surely this trade is already represented more than sufficiently. Experience has shown that wherever it succeeds in dominating our public bodies, public spirit sinks to a low level, and corruption creeps in. Surely this is not the time to allow such an influence to become paramount in our town. We have just recovered from one disgrace. Surely we shall not be so short sighted and so foolish as to allow ourselves to be dragged into another. In your Ward. ex-Councillor Rogers is attempting to oust Councillor Wilding, whose most satisfactory work and votes, during his short membership (as set forth in his Election address) should, apart from any other con- sideration, secure for his candidature the unhesitating support of every free and independent elector. Judging from his election address, the past public record of Mr. Rogers, so far as any i useful work what- ever for the people is concerned, s a complete blank. During my time he was a bitter and an offensive oppo- nent of every effort of the Non-Traders. The only tangible result of his connection with the Council that I am aware of is that his son holds a post in the electricity department. But it is as a beer-seller' and as the tied-house tenant of a drink-shop, that I ask you to prevent Mr. Rogers' return to the Council. Drink is the enemy, the bane of our family life, the source of almost all our crime, and the greatest hindrance to the work of the Christian Churches. True there are Christian clergymen who gladly receive the moneys of the brewers, and who are not ashamed to support drink-selling candidates for public office. We must not judge these men : hut we shall ourselves be judged, in our consciences, according to our own conceptions of conduct and duty. T think it was Lord Rosebery who recently said that if the English nation did not shortly strangle the drink demon, the drink demon would strangle the English nation. If this be so,and who can truthfully deny it—l strongly appeal to you that, as you value your good name and the fair fame of the town and of the country you live in, you will not on Friday next give your vote or influence to any man who comes before Ton as a representative of his demoralising and corrupting trade.—l trust you may see your way, for the reasons I have given, to record your vote for Nfr. Wilding. The wise citizen of to-day must sink party ties in Municipal Elections, and must vote for the man who has no private or trade interests to serve, and I cannot but believe, as I earnestly pray, that you will be guided when you are marking your voting paper, to so mark it, that you may thereafter be proud of, and thank God, for the vote you have recorded—one more vote against self interest—and for the public weal.

T am, Sir or Madam, Your faithful and obedient Servant, JAMES DODDS.

COUNCIL AND PROPERTY OWNERS. £12,000 Required for Street Improvement.

Mr. R. H. BICKNELL, inspector of the Local Government Board, held an inquiry at the Town Hall on Thursday morning into the application of the Corpora- tion for sanction to borrow £12,926 Gs. 9d. for works of private street improvement, under the Private Street Works Act, 1892. The proceedings were of a formal character, not a single member of the general public attending. The TowN CLERK (Mr. J. Lyon Whittle) made the application on behalf of the Corporation and submitted the usual particulars as to the population and financial position of the Borough. The outstanding debt at present is £1,167,025; and the population is estimated at £70,000. The Council, said Mr. Whittle, had decided to postpone the paving of the passage on the west side of the Bolton Council School, the cost of which was estimated at £2lB 7s. sd.That reduced the amount of the application to £12,707 18s. 4d. Five objections were lodged against the works being undertaken, but all had been withdrawn. In answer to the inspector, Mr. A. M. Ker (assistant borough surveyor) said none of the work had been done. The BOROUGH TREASURER (Mr. J. Fairhurst) told the inspector that the Corporation was in somewhat of a difficulty. The Board granted them seven years for repayment of loans and the Corporation allowed property owners seven years in which to repay them. The Corporation paid for all the material and the work done and it practically meant that they borrowed the , money twelve months before the apportionments were made, and eight years elapsed before they were repaid by the property owners. If the Board would extend the period of repayment to eight years the Corporation would be able to keep within the limit. The INSPECTOR: The cure for that is to give the property owners six years in which to pay. Take seven years with the intention of giving them six. The BOROUGH TREASURER: We should have an uprising among the property owners of Warrington. ' The IKSPECTOR : Has it been the practice always to give them seven years? The BOROUGH TREASURER : No. We gave them three at first and they applied for a longer time, so we extended it to seven. The INSPECTOR: At present there is no idea of extending the period beyond the seven veers. The usual practice in other towns is to give the property owners one year less than you receive for repayment. There would no doubt be a little bit of outcry at the commencement hut if you treated everybody in a similar manner and gave them the reasons, I don't see why they should object. The BoRoUGH TREASURER: We have kept within the limit hitherto by one or two owners paying in advance of the period allowed. The INSPECTOR: 'My experience—and it is a some- what long one—when I ask the treasurer's in other towns is that they inform me that the majority of people rather than have the naoney hang on. pay up. The BOROUGH TREASURER: We don't find them so here. The INSPECTOR. : Probably they are small crvners I and it, is much harder for them to pay than the large builders. The BOROUGH TREASURER : Ours are mostly property speculators and they build property and try to get rid of it. People scrape the money together to buy six or eight cottages.— The INSPECTOR : And they have not been told of the liability on the road ? The BOROUGH TREASURER : That is so. We find many cases of that kind. Then comes our difficulty in getting the money. The TNsPECToR : They will see from the reports of this inquiry the position they are in when they bnv , houses in a street which has not be made. They will I know that they will have to pay approximately 18s. a foat over and above the money they paid for the houses. can hold nut, no hope that you will get more than seven years. THE GRAMMAR SCHOOL.

Appointment of a New Headmaster.

A Good Scholar and Cricketer.

The Governors of the Warrington Grammar School met on Thursday to appoint a headmaster in succession to the late Rev. E. J. Willcocks, MA. Originally there were 46 applications for the post and at preliminary meetings these were reduced to seven, and afterwards to three. On Thursday the governors unanimously appointed the. Rev. Horace Gray, M.A., headmaster of Kendal Grammar School, and some time assistant master at Wellington college, to the offiee. The salary is 1.600 per annum. The Rev. Horace Gray was educated at Jesus College, Cambridge, holding the senior open mathe- matical scholarship, and being placed 21st wrangler in the mathematical tripos of that year. Each year during his residence at Cambridge he was awarded college prizes for proficiency in mathematies. He proceeded to the degree of MA. in 1000. After graduating, Mr. Gray was appointed senior mathematical master at the United Services College, Westward Ho! where be remained two years (1897-1898,)during which period his pupils were most successful in the competitive examinations for Woolwich and Sandhurst, as also in the usual University entrance examinations. He resigned his post at Westward Ho! in order to take Holy Orders, becoming licensed to the parish of Littleham,Bideford, near Devon. He was ordained deacon at the Advent ordination in 1898 and priest a year later by the Lord Bishop of Exeter. He held his cure for two years (1899-1900) when on the resignation of his Rector, the Rev. H. G. Moore. MA., he accepted a mastership at Ipswich school. During his stay at Ipswich (1901-2) his pupils su-ceeded in obtaining open mathematical scholarship, dis- tinction at Cambridge, and passed the competitive entrance examination for the Royal Navy, in addition to the London Matriculation and Higher Certificate examination of the Oxford and Cambridge Board. In January. 190$, Mr. Gray removed from Ipswich to undertake mathematical and science teaching at Wellington College, in addition to which, he was, during the early part of his • Mastership there, responsible for the usual Form Subjects. He remained at Wellington College until July, 1904, when be was unanimously invited, from a field of 85, by the Governors of Kendal School to undertake his present charge. During Mr. Gray's headmastership the school produced some excel- lent work. The number of scholars in attendance had I been more than fully maintained. His first annual Ireport to the Governors proved that there was an ' increase in the School Roll, which had not. its equal in the preceding twelve pears. This confidence had continued, and flr the present term the number of new names added exceeded the total entrance for the whole of the last school year. With reference to examination results, forty of his boys have sat for the Cambridge Local Examinations, and nearly all have passed. Others have taken the usual pieliuninary examinations, and four of his senior boys, and the only ones who have sought such distinc- tion, have obtained open exhibitions at Cambridge. Kendal Grammar School is subject to the regulations of the Board of Education, and the report of a recent full inspection was highly gratifying, and testified to the vigorous tone of school life there. The fiancial position and administration of the School, has so considerably improved during Mr. Gray's adminis- tion, that an adverse balance of some £2OO has been entirely swept away, in addition to many improvements having been made to the School premises. Mr. Gray takes a keen interest in the external life of the school. He obtained his Cricket Blue at Cambridge, playing against Oxford in the years 1894 and 1895. In the summer vacations he has regularly assisted his County in the Minor Counties' Competition, and captained the eleven during three seasons. This experience has stood him in good stead in Kendal, for, when free from school duties, he has been able to assist the local club in their League games. Mr. Gray was born in November, 1874, and married in 1905 the third daughter of the Rev. C. It. Wood, Rector of Bredfield, Suffolk.

Woods' Great Peppermint Cure, for Coughs and Colds; never fails. ITh, 2/9.—Advt.

CORONER AND SOLICITOR. LIVELY EXCHANGES.

At an inquest held recently at Widnes Coroner Brighouse said that his Majesty's Inspector had refused to be present because he had not had the twenty-four hours' notice required by law, and he intimated to the court that he would have to postpone it until Monday, at a quarter-past nine. Mr. Knowles, who eppeared for the United Alkali Company, for whom deceased (David Stapleton) worked, said :—" One expects courts to sit during business hours." This raised the Coroner's ire, and he asked whether 9.15 was not within proper business hours, demanding that Mr. Knowles should apologise before he again endeavoured to address the court. The court assembled at 9.15, and Mr. Knowles was present. After the first witness had been called, the Coroner asked Mr. Thomas (for the relatives) and Mr. Evans (his Majesty's Inspector) if they had any questions to ask. Mr. Knowles : Am I not allowed to ask questions? The Coroner : Your position is quite clear. I told you on the last occasion if you express your regret for what you said to the court I shall allow you. If you don't, I won't. Mr. Knowles : Well, I am afraid I cannot express my regret, having expressed that I thought a quarter- past nine too early ; and I have been inconvenienced in getting here this morning. The Coroner: If you cannot express your regret, ' your position is that you have no legal standing. Only myself, the jury, and his !‘fajesty's inspector have any right conferred upon thein by statute; other people are here simply by my courtesy. Mr, Knowles : If my client is warned to attend I think I am entitled to be here, but not to ask questions. The Coroner: I shall not recognise you, Mr Knowles, until you say you regret— Mr. Knowles (interrupting) : I feel the hour is early. The Coroner : If you are prepared to do -- Mr. Knowles (again interrupting) : I cannot express my regrot as I have been inconvenienced. The Coroner : Then I cannot recognise you this morning ; as this has been said in cold blood, I shall not recognise you on any future occasion. If you ex- press regret I shall forget all about it. Mr. Knowles: I do not want your forgiveness. I have done nothing wrong. The Coroner: lam not offering you forgiveness. I don't hear you, Mr. Knowles. Mr. Knowles : Very well, then ; leave it at that. The inquest was held on the body of David Stapleton, aged fifty-three years, a chemical labourer, residing in Charlotte-street. On the 10th inst. he received a scratch on his elbow whilst working at a salt-cake furnace. Blood-poisoning set in, and he died on the 19th inst. The jury returned a verdict of "Accidental death," caused by blood-poisoning, set up by the injury to the elbow.

T. HELENS.

HELP FOR ST. HELENS HOSPITALS.

The committee of the St. Helens charity cricket match and sports have this year raised over LUG on behalf of the local hospitals. The money was divided on Tuesday evening, and handed over by the Mayor (Alderman Bishop)to the treasurers of the hospitals, £35 each being given to the St. He!ens lnd the Providence Hospitals and the Nurses' Institute, and £7 to the Fresh Air Fund, which leaves a saaall balance in hand.

WATCHMAN'S FATAL SEIZURE.. Roger Hughes, aged seventy-three of Parr-street, died in the Providence Hospital, St. Helens, early on Tues- day under somewhat extraordinary circurnstarces. Hughes was watchman at Mr. Melling's stoneyard on the canal bank. At 8.30 on \fondly night Mr. Melling found him unconscious in his cabin. He bad previously complained of feeling unwell. Dr. Bailey found Hughes suffering from a fit, and had him taken to hospital where he died four hours later, evidently from natural causes.

WIFE DESERTION AT ST. HELENS.

John M`Queeney, a chemical labourer, who had been arrested at Bolton, was sentenced by the St. Helens magistrates on Monday to two months' hard labour for deserting his wife and family. Relieving Officer Richardson said M`Queeney had a good job in St. Helens, but early in September be left his family destitute. The Clerk said the man had been proceeded against six times for desertion. Prisoner said he was coming back to take his wife out of the workhouse, but the bench declined to believe this,and sent him to gaol. SUNDAY GAMBLING AT ST. HELENS.

Hector Allen, a young man, of Prospect-road, Parr pleaded guilty at St. Helens, on Monday, to Sunday, gambling. Police-constable Anders said Allen was one of a gambling gang. Vc'hen they were disturbed he found 2s. 7d. on the floor. Allen.—No, you never did. You took that out of my hand. The Clerk.—Didn't he give you his name and address when you locked him up? The Chief Consttible said it was necessary to lock these men up. It was the only way they would be able to do any good with them. The police had power of arrest under the Vagrancy Act. Allen was fined 10s. and costs, or fourteen days. ANTI-SOCIALIST MEETING AT ST. HELENS. A Conservative election meeting, which was described as an anti-Socialist demonstration, was held on Tuesday at St. Helens Town Hall. Colonel W. Lee Pilkington presided over a large attendance, who had been admitted most carefully by ticket, with the view of keeping out I disturbers. The Chairman said that for some time I past a certain party had been assailing the Town Hall, and trying to wrest the seats in the Council from those who for so many years had charge of the town's affairs. The town had been carried on by a Council composed of Conservatives and Liberals, and ever since the borough was incorporated it had been exceedingly ' well managed, and it was due to the skilful way in which the town's affairs had been managed that St. Helens was the lowest rated town of its size in the kingdom. (Applause.) Look at places that had been ruled by the Socialists—Poplar and West Ham ! The rate in Poplar were 12s. in the poundoind it was as near bankruptcy as it possibly could be. Colonel Richard Pilkington moved, "That this meet- ing of St. Helens ratepayers records its confidence in the present careful administration of the town's affairs, and strongly condemns the attempt of the Socialistic party to obtain control of our municipality." Colonel Pilkington dealt with the figures relating to the administration of the town, and rehearsed the names of the men who, like the late Sir David Gamble, had worked early and late for the highest good. They came before the electors that night to ask for their approval of what their party had done, tlnd to urge them to support the six candidates that the party had put before them. Alderman J. Massey seconded, and said that Socialism would destroy civil and religious liberty and would destroy their manufactures and prevent other manufactures from coming to the town. —Mr. Rigby Swift supported, and denied that the employment of the unemployed and the feeding of children could be called Socialism. It was common- sense, common Christianity, and as soon as the genius 1 of man had devised a way of doing it, it would be done. In the same way, he would be in favour of old-age pensions as soon as some statesman had introduced a , scheme by which pensions could be given to those who tdeserved them.—Mr. R. J. Mucartney also supported, and the resolution was unanimously carried, and a resolution pledging the meeting to secure the return of the Conservative oandidates was also secured.

EARLESTOWN AND NEWTON.

BURNING FATALITY AT HAYDOCK.

On 11;iO-tiday a sad fatality occurred at Haydock. Ethel Case, aged four months, the daughter of Zachariah Case, 342, Church-road, Haydock, was placed about four feet from the fire. By some unexplained accident the child's pinafore became ignited, and she sustained such injuries that she died.

NEWTON CONSERVATITE AGENT AND MR. W. G. WELLS. Mr. J. H. Bottomley, Conservative agent, writes to "The Times" from Newton-le-Willows :—I see Mr. H. G. Wells calls attention to an error in my pamphlet on" Socialism, Atheism, and Free Love." Be quotes the second edition (the only one of 15 where the mis- take occurs), and it has been explained locally by the printers. In resettinz, a quotation mark got one line below where it ought to have appeared and where it has been printed in most of the 50,000 copies issued.

OUR LOCAL CHURCHES.

Rev. T. Galland Hartley at Bold-street Church. The Rev. T. Galland Hartley, chairman of the district, preached to large congregations at Bold-street Wesleyan Church on Sunday. In tte evening the rev. gentleman based his discourse on St. John xx. 2S-29. The subject was the incredulity and con- fession of Thomas, who was described as tender- hearted, affectionate but cautious, and as one who must 'be convinced before he advanced. He was a man who was prone to dwell in the shadows, and who did not easily come into the sunlight of faith—a man who was represented by the old artists as sometimes having a measuring rod in hts hand with which to measure the way before he trod, and by others as the man with the scales with which to weigh all the atgaments adduced to his reason. Some of the lessons to be learnt from Thomas were given, such as the ultimate triumph of faith over doubt. There were some whose doubts were rather of the head than of the heart. They had the wish to believe, but their temperament, constitution, and environment could not advance in the light as quickly as others could. They must advance cautiously. The preacher. advised people when in doubt not to stay in the cold world, where their doubts would be deepened and be likely to lead to unbelief, and at last deapair, but to go into the warm glowing atmosphere of the church, where Christ would breath upon them His peace. Rev. H. Dunnico at Warrington, The Rev. H. Dunnico, pastor of Kensington Church, Liverpool, formerly of the Golborne-street Baptist Church, visited Warrington on Sunday and addressed the members of the Warrington P.S.A. Society in the afternoon, in the Co-operative Hall. There was a large attendance of members and friends. In the evening Mr. Duntiico preached in the Parr Hall before a crowded congregation. The fundamental principle of Christian faith, said the preacher, was self-sacrifice. Though he sometimes felt that the Church had as yet to learn the simplest element of the Christian faith, they wanted to remember that the Christian life was not a picnic—it was a bloody crusade, it was a great warfare. The Church sat at ease and the people perished. Why was the Church of God to-day so impotent,so helpless in the face of the great evils which confronted them? Because she had never learnt to suffer,could never hope to raise. Step by step they mounted the throne to the Cross. Sometimes they were told that the Church, or rather the ministers of the day, were not quite so orthodox as the ministers of a past generation. They were told from some quarters that the modern ministers fought somewhat shy of the doctrine of the Atonement. They were told that a new heresy had crept into the Christian Church, and he (Mr. Dunnico) was not prepared to deny it, but a great amount of the criticism levelled at the so-called new theology was due dinctly to a misunderstand- ing of the cardinal principles of the new theology. If there be any truth in the accusation that the modern pulpit was growing somewhat lax in its proclamation of the Gospel of Atonement—the principle of sacrifice, living and dying one for another—the remarkable thing was this : That the world of science was becoming wonderfully orthodox. What they %ranted to emphasize to-day was that the Church could never become a powerful force, a manifesto of the spirit of Jesus Christ until they learnt the simplest element of the Christian doctrine—the law of self-sacrifice. What the world lacked was motive power to realize ideals—the motive power given by Christ of Calvary. For nineteen hundred years they had been preaching the Gospel of Jesus Christ ; they had laid a foundation that was sure. Never since the world began had the youth of any 'age had such glorious opportunities as to-day, and he appealed to them to embrace them. The Kingdom of Cod was coming. Miss Bancroft; mezzo-soprano, effectively sang "The Holy City," and Mr. E. Walker's bass voice was heard to advantage in" Jerusalem " whilst Mr. Bates contri- buted a finished interpretation of the difficult violin solo, "Air d' Eglise." Mr. A. Trow was the organist. The collections, afternoon and evening, were in aid of the society's funds.

Bewsey-road Wesleyan Church. Special services were held at this churh on Sunday, the occasion being the Foreign Missionary Anniversary. There were large congregations both morning and evening The preacher on Sunday was the Rev. A. Cecil Parker formerly-erg this circuit, whose stirring discourses were listened to with great interest. On Monday a public meeting took place. Mr. C. E. Parker presided and was supported by the Revs. Avon Walton and Sidney Pitt, a retired missionary from South Africa. During the evening a story of Tulsi Ramyia " was narrated by several young ladies from Lymm who were attired in Elindoo costume. Mr. C. E. Parker expressed his sincere sympathy with the cause that they had met to support and hoped they would have a very successful evening. (Applause.) Missionary work no doubt needed supporting as their were thousands and millons of souls in the foreign lands which needed converting and without missionaries it could not be done. He hoped they would gain by Mr. Walton's address and he asked Mr. Pitt if he would kindly take the chair as he (Mr, Parker) had to fill another important engagement elsewhere and he was very sorry he had to go. Mr. PITT took the chair and briefly introduced Mr. Walton to the audience. The Rev. AVON WALTO'N, who was received with applause, said he was very pleased to be present not only to speak to them but to visit Lancashire. Warrington was an old Lancashire town, and if there was anything he liked to cheer him up it was a Lancashire dialect. (Laughter.) Lancashire ought not to be ashamed of the work it had done in Rhodesia. The people of Rhodesia were very superstitious, and if anything happened they always believed that it had been caused by evil spirits. They believed that all trees, stones and cocks, and everything in fact, contained thousands of evil spitits. Whenever anything wrong happened, they always sent for witch doctor. He was called the" smeller out," a man . who was always where he had no business to be. The witch doctors had a great deal of power in their hands, They were very cruel and sometimes almost dementede and yet in the heathen hearts of the country the people had an implicit confidence in them. Although Rhodesia was now under the British Government, he could assur` them that those things were still carried on, but they were now criminal offences. In 1893 and 1896 they had two native rebellions, and they were both caused by the witch doctors. The witch doctors, except those who , had been converted, hated the missionaries as if they were poison.

===== 0002601_19071102_art0068.txt | included=true | included =====
BAPTIST CHURCH LATCHFORD.

Annual Sale of Work.

The annual sale of work in connection with this Church took place in the schoolroom, Wash lane, on Thursday afternoon. There was a very good attendance and much praise is due to the stallholders for the effective manner in which they had arranged and decorated the stalls. 'The sale was opened by Mr. James Dodds and the Rev. J. F. Matthews presided. The stalls were in charge of the following :—ltefresh- ment stall, Mrs. Wilkinson ; dairy produce stall, Mrs. J. Fairhurst; plain stall, Mrs. W. Adams; fancy stall, Mrs. A. Albinson ; flower stall,'Mrs. F. W. Grounds; and grocery stall, Mr. J. R. Conway. The Rev. J. F. MATTH.Ews said their church was supported by voluntuy offerings. The income they required became greater and greater every year because of their increased expenditure. There were various , items of expenditure which they could not make up with their ordinary income and that was why they had .2this sale of work. They hoped in a very short period to have a piano in their schoolroom for use at their social gatherings, and that piano would be second to none. (Laughter and applause.) There had been a considerable amount of expenditure during the past twelve months. They had made their church a great deal brighter from the outside, and they had a neat little notice board as well as sundry other things. He extended a hearty welcome to Mr. and Mrs. Dodds, and he had great pleasure in announcing that Mr. Dodds had handed him a cheque for £lO towards their sale and £2 for other purposes. (Applause.) The congregation of their Church had got together, and by strenuous work had saved the Church £5O. (Applause.) He thanked the stallholders for their efforts, and he would be obliged to Mr. Dodds if he would declare the sale open. (Applause.) Mr. JAMES pOpDS said he had great pleasure in accepting the invitation of Mr. Matthews to come and declare the sale open. There must be good and kind i people n the neighbourhood of Latchford or else they would not have had such a great many stalls and stall - holders, and they would not have had such beautiful work on the stalls. (Applause.) They must have worked very hard indeed for months. He thanked Mr. Matthews for his kind remarks and hoped he would be long spared to enjoy his position as minister to the Latchford Baptists. There had never been a better preacher at that church since it bad been opened. (Applause.) He hoped their sale of work would be* great success and that they would dispose of all their goods and therefore realise a substantial sum. He had great pleasure in declaring the sale open. (Applause.) The sale realised the very satisfactory sum of £56.

LANCIISHIRE COMINATION.

THE VOICE OF LABOUR.

Edited by John Workman.

Readers of this column are invited to assist in making it interesting and helpful to social workers by contributing themselves. Correspondents must write on one side of the paper only, and endeavour to make their letters and notes short and to the point. A nom-de-plume may be used, but the real name of the. writer should be forwarded in confidence to the Editor of the column. M.S.S. must be addressed to John Workman, " Examiner " Office, Warrington and should be sent in not later than Wednesday in each week. _

===== 0002601_19071102_art0075.txt | included=true | included =====
crffi ISTIANITY AND SOCIALISM.

John Workman's protest against my charge of mis- representation would have been far more effective if be had shown wherein I was wrong in saying that he had •again and again misrepresented what I bad said and given to my words a meaning which they could not possibly _convey.

John Wo-rkman In speaking to Mr. S. Webb's repudia- tion of any affinity between Christian and Modern Socialism says " He [C. E. P.] evidently prefers Mr. Webb's interpretation of Christian Socialism rather than that of Mr. Maurice himself" This is scarcely a fair way of putting it. Mr. Web has no quarrel with Maurice's description of Christian Socialism; he simply points out the essential difference between it and Modern Socialism and objects to Christian Socialism because it preserves the voluntary character of association, allows ample play and reward for individual effort, and condemns State control. I quite acknowledge (what may really be my opponent's point, although he does not make it very clear) that Christian Socialism and Modern Socialism spring from the same motive, a desire to help the condition .of those who suffer under pressnt unfair conditions. The sweated tailors whom the Christian Socialists sought to unite in Co-operative Societies have their counterpart in present day condi- tions and the object of modern Socialism is to aid all who have not a fair chance or individual ability to raise themselves unaided. My point and the one emphasised by Mr. Webb is that the methods of the two systems are fundamentally different. If John Workman is agreed in this we need not labour this aspect of the question, but can rule it out and so shorten the discussion.

John Workman objects to my definition of what he stands for: "giving to each according to his need without any reference to his faithfulness or energy." He tells us that the latter part has emanated from my own brain and is no part of the scheme he advocates. It would have helped to a termination of this pro- longed controversy if in repudiating ray interpretation of his scheme, be had told us what his scheme really was. In this he has been singularly vague and reticent both in what be writes himself and in hie voluminous quotations. This is the first time we have the slightest indication that his plan of "giving to everyone according to his need and from everyone according to his ability" was subject to any exceptions and limitations. And even now we are left to conclude, only by inference, that faithfulness and energy are to be considered in the division of the products of industry. I concluded, not unreasonably, that if a man is to get according to his need, any other factor which will conflict with this standard must be ruled out. Surely it is clear that if effective energy put forth by the individual is to be considered in his reward then the other standard of giving to each according to his need will be thrown into helpless confusion. If my opponent holds that the Committee (or whatever all- win authority is to make the division) is to allocate acoording to faithfulness and energy, he is taking the first step on the high road to the adoption of the prin- ciple for which I contend that a man is entitled to demand the full and proportionate value of what he produces. There may, therefore, not be so much difference between John Workman and myself as this controversy has indicated. At present, however, it is difficult to know where our friend stands and there- fore it is useless for me to pursue this aspect of the question_ further.

- My children are playing a gade of biding a thimble. The seekers wander about the room looking at every spot where the thimble might rest. After a little time, if they are unsuccessful, the hider aids them by indicating that so-and-so is getting "warm." They all gather mund the" warm" one and soon a shout of glee proclaims the object found. I feel at last after weeks of inconclusive controversy we are at last beginning to get warm. There seems a chance that at length John Workman is prepared to face the difficulty that I have again and again raised of how his main principle of paying a man according to his needs can be harmonized with the necessary recognition of faithfulness and energy which he now seems to admit must be taken into account. I am sorry if I have misunderstood our friend and to avoid further confusion will he tell us in plain language what principles are to rule the reward a man is to get r his labour in the Socialistic State he desires to establish and how these principles can be harmonized in their application to the question of division ? And may we further ask what plan be proposes to adopt so make those do their fair share who show neither faith fulness nor energy. John Workman must excuse my dealing with his customary batch of quotations. I would have no objection to taking up with Mr. Sidney Webb the points, which are by the way very familiar, if I were agreeing with that gentleman. At present lam deal- ing with John Workman and the important thing for the present is to get to know what form of Socialism —or is it Communism? - he himself really advocates. Therefore an examination into the many questionable assertions be introduces in such profusion from well- known writers may be left out of the discussion. Replying to " Student's " question as to what I 'mean by "Compulsory State Socialism," in view of the proposals made by the Socialist party I thought these words would explain themselves. The proposed conditions are the antithesis of the voluntary associa- tions formed by the Christian Socialists. There was a time in our island story when everyone was obliged to attend the Established Church and no other. This was the compulsory State form of worship. A man's religion was decided by the majority and freedom of will and conscience were dethroned. No doubt in many things the majority must rule' but there are elementary individnal rights which no majority has any title to invade; how, and where, and if at all, a man shall worship a God is one of these. A man's first claim to the possession of what by his labour he produces appcars to me to be another. A majority, however large has no right to take by compulsion the result of anyone's labour and hand it over to another. We readily admit that society has a claim to a percentage of the result of an, individual's labour in exchange for the protection and advantages which it affords to all its members. The taxes we pay for police protection, for the upkeep of roads, for the mrintenance of law by which justice is maintained ' between man and man, for means of national defence, I are legitimate charges which society can justly claim 1 fromits members. This principle can be carried ] further without wronging the individual. For ex-ample, it has been found desirable for the safety of tl:e nation to undertake the education of the children because a rising generation untaught and undisciplined 1 mould become a danger to the we.ll-being of the State. It may be found necessary to extend this principle in many directions and for like reasons. But this is an entirely different thing from the compulsory. State application of a principle which severs the connection , between effort and its, natural proportionate reward as hitherto advocated by 3( ho Workman. But I must now wait till the Editor reveals what he is contending for, for in very truth I do not at present know. ,

CHARtES E. PARKER

Penketb, October 31st, 1907.

• Sir,—l have been an interested student of the con- tributions penned by MI. Charles E Parker and John Workman, which have recently been published in "The Voice of Labour" dealing with the subject "Christ and Socialism." Whilst lam in complete pgreement with some of the statements made by each gentleman, unfortunately I am at variance with both disputants on the main points at is:sue., In the first place the ideals of Socialism 'and Christianity appear to me neither identical nor antago- nistic. Socialists desire a clemccratic co-operative industrial conrinonwealkh founded on the socialization of land and capital, with the removal of all class and sex inequalities, which would, (they claim), per- manently solve the poverty problem and the unemployed evil, and entirely abolfsh destitution, pauperism and ignorance. Christians aim at the regeneration of the individual by means of the Divine and humanising influence ascribed by them to the teachings and life of the lowly Nazarene. Socialism is a system of Government' and has (I claim) no closer relationship with Christianity than Toryism and Liberalism. The Socialist move- ment appeals to men and women connected with all religious creeds and sects irrespective of nationality, colour or race, whilst both the Liberal and Conserva-

===== 0002601_19071102_art0076.txt | included=false | skipped/gap: fewer than 20 non-control characters after cleanup =====


===== 0002601_19071102_art0077.txt | included=true | included =====
tive parties contain many Christians,lnfidels,Agnostics and Unitarians.

Then Mr. Parker claims that everyone should receive the" whole "fruits of his (or her) labour. I kankly confess, Sir, that I have entirely failed to discoAr any prospective substantial social advantage that could possibly be secured by the universal adoption of this proposed reform (so called) which appears to me both impracticable and unjust. Can Mr. Parker (or any of his- followeis) give an approximate estimate of the relative incomes that would be paid (under the suggested system) to clerics and scavengers, policemen and stokers, lamplighters and soldiers, seamstresses and barmaids? Is it possible to definitely state what are "the whole fruits of the 'abettor" performed by the persons engaged in these particular occupations? If the workers are to receive the whole fruits of their labour, what will be the fate of all unfortunate men and women, who, through mental or physical weakness, are unable to produce the requisite "fruits" to procure for themselves the bare necessaries of life? Surely Mr. Parker doesn't believe that the young children and industrial veterans have not a legitimate claim on Society for their support and maintenance, and I sincerely hope that he is not an advocate of child-labour and opposed to old-age pensions. Are there any of the recognised leaders of the great Liberal party, who are, in the slightest degree, sympathetic to the ideal advocated by the respected President of the Warrington Liberals, that "each worker should receive the whole fruits of his labour?" On the other hand John Workman's alternative to Mr. Parker's proposals reads thus "from each according to his capacity, to each according to his needs." Will John Workman please inform us who will be the authority appointed to decide on each individual's " capacity "for work, and under his high- flown utopian scheme will every person be permitted to take from the common-stock everything that he (in his opinion) needs, say for example, such trifles as motor cars, pianos, billiard-tables, bowling-greens, parks, etc. In conclusion I trust that there will be a prompt and satisractory reply given to my queries, for I feel assured that on the subject of social reform, there are hundreds of your readers who, like your humble servant, are sorely

LIBERAL CLUD.--The other evening the final of a billiard handicap took place at the atove club. The two opponents were Messrs. Harold Chatham and Smith- Tunstall, both being scratch men, who treated the spectators to an exciting game. Dr. Harold Joseph Withdraws His Resignation.

PENKETH.

Mr DEAR CHILD:

I have been most pleased with the let received during the last week ;'-..,0re than twice the number r usually receii Well, 1 expect you get more time now the long evenings are here. I find some of you have already sent me anecdotes of your pets; but I want a lot more; don't forget, I shall try and find a nice box of Fry's chocolates for the best received. I will mention again the subjects of our cont. petitions. I want your special attention given to these.

First. I want you to remember that our poor people will soon be needing some of those warm articles that you have promised to send me.

Secondly. The competition is open for the best anecdotes of your pet animals. ' Thirdly. The largest list of new members before the year is out. Which will you try for? All of them, I hope. With love,

UNCLE NED.

A PROUD FROST PRINCESS.

There was once a proud little icicle, who stood alone out in the cold. She wore a dress that sparkled like diamonds, but, for all that, no one cared to go near her. The snowflakes were having a game of tag in the sky. Nearer and nearer the earth they played, until some of them spied Miss Icicle. "Do come and play with us," they cried, but the proud icicle shook her head. s" No," she said; "you are entirely too common to play with me." "I'll show the world what you are, you silly thing," called Grandfather Sun from his cloud chariot. So he sent some of his children, the sunbeams, to breathe their hot breath on Miss Icicle's head. This made her feel so sick that she wept great tears.' The more she wept the thinner she, grew, until at last a tiny pool of water was all that was left of the princess.

NELLiE VOUND.

THE MOUSE AND THE LION.

There was once a little mouse who thought. he would have a walk in the forest, when he came across a lion, which began to roar. The little mouse thought he was going to be eaten up, but, instead of that, the lion let him go. About two days after the mouse went another walk, and he saw the same lion again, which was caught in a hunter's net, and so the little mouse. gnawed away the rope, and the lion was set free. The mouse and the lion.were friend!, for a long time. Kind deedi are always 'repaid. D. TALBOT.

Ralph and Rob had been reading stories about the great explorers, and they had come to the conclusion that they had found the right occupation for life. They would be explorers, and make maps of the countries they dl6 covered.

"The only trouble is there are no places round here which people don't know about," complained Ralph as they lay in bed, trying to decide how they would begin the business of opening up, unknown territory. "My plan is this," said the practical Rob. "You see everything has to be practised before it amounts to much—like playing the piano or learning to be an engineer. So I think we ought to practise exploring round the mill-pond and in the pasture until right down we know whether we can stand it to camp out and—all that _sort of thing."

Ralph agreed to this, and they decided to begin operations at once. The next day they set about building a raft. They brought heavy logs from the pasture; their father had allowed them to cut down some of the small trees bor- dering the cow-path. It was quite hard work to drag the logs to the shed, and they found scme difficulty in getting the raft to the pond after tit was completed. Their next trouble was the raft was so heavy and uneven that they were unable to keep it above water when they were both on it. Perhaps it would bo best to say nothing about the accusations and herd words that were said about this matter, but it ended with their abandoning the raft and de- ciding that they would begin with an explora- tion on land.

Uncle Jack happened to pass the pcn:l v.-1)1Ic the trouble was gcing on. He bci.:l aL•rig:ll idea.

"Boys, you can do better than You can be a real village improvement society. Now you see this part of the sidc,,.-wall; here is low and wet, and makes an for any one coming along: in the Let's make a bridge. Let's take that raft a - ,rt lay it, on some rocks and make a road-bed, aLd let us cover it all over with stones and earth until we have raised that. hollow tin the road."

" @mat!" said both boys, who always fcund Uncle Jack's plan the best kind of play. ThQr straightway began to pull-the affair, which was a failure as a raft, toward the shore. They all worked with a will, bringing flat tones and packing them down solidly wizh tte earth until the hollow place was filled. Ncxt they placed the raft on the stones, then cover.?(l all with layers of earth and gravel. They left Inderneath a little drain for the water to pass through. When all was completed they had a neat, permanent side-walk.

The strangest part of this sthry is thio. The logs of the raft were of willow, and were wf;ter- soaked when laid. A sprout soon appcarod at one end of the log, which, trained •1r..7:1;r1y. grew higher ea,th year, until a gracof:.l tree hung over the side walk and made a bit ot grateful shade. "We are more of an improvement s.'7ir‘ty, than we meant to be," said Ralph, day some years later.

"Yes, I guess we did as much goci as we would have in exploring the upper end of tte pond," said Rob.

A MORTIFYING MISTAKE.

I studied my tables over and over, and back- ward and forward, too, But I couldn't remember six times nine, and I didn't know what to do, Till sister told me to play with my doll and not to bother my head; "If you call her fifty-four for a while, you'll learn it by heart," she said. So I took my favourite Mary Ann (tlicur,rh •-

thought 'twas a dreadful shame . To give such a perfectly lovely child such a • perfectly horrid name), 4, And I called her my dear little fifty-four & . . . .. .... _ _

- hundred times till I knew - The answer of six times nine as well as tile

answer of two times two. Next day Elizabeth Wigglesworth, who always acts so proud, Said, "Six times nine is fifty-two," and I nearly laughed aloud! But I wish I hadn't when teacher said, "Now, Dorothy, tell, if you can." For I thought of my doll, and—sakes alive ! I answered, "Mary Ann."

There was once a little girl who was studying cookery. One day her father asked her if she could make a suet pudding. "Oh, yes," sbc replied, "you just try me." So ht-r iatncr bought her a bag of flour and some suet, alici told her to try her best. When dinner-tinie came the father wished to cut the ruddi "Why, Mary," he exclaimed, "this is a funny pudding; I can't find the suet." "Go on cut- ting," said Mary, "and you will find it," and, behold, she had put the lump in the middle.

AN INCIDENT IN THE FRANCO-GERMAN . WAR. YOU cannot. tell, who safely dwell Far. from the storm of war, What strength these nursing sisters bring, How much to us they are. One such we had in our rough troop, Therese, her nursing name; Saine said at home they called her Rose, We knew not whence she came.

n she was near we lost our fear, Such power did she possess; Her voice was like a benison, Which made our pain seem less. \-c 0 grew ashamed to show our pain, Or mount the sign of fear; Ve learned to stifle down our groans, And laugh out, "C'est pour rire." cine day the fight was fierce and long; Some thirty soldiers lay `‘Vounded and spent in the Lazare tent, Near to the hottest fray. When, lo a shell among us fell From out the battle mist, And brave me D fled, while near our bed The deadly fionster hissed. But sister, dear, brave "C'est pour rire," Quick stooped to where it lay, Lifted the threatening engine up, And staggered, pale, away. A hundred yards she struggled on, Each step new danger meant; A failing hand, a faulty int, And life and limb are rent. Her trembling hands then sought to lay Their burden on the ground; 114-,o great! it slipped, fell earthward—

0 God I that hateful sound. It shook our tents, it shook the earth, It shook our hearts yet more; Pale faces looked forth questioning, Lips wordless, at the door. From her white brow they wiped the red, They stooped her words to hear: "No! not yet dead!" she faintly said, Then, smiling, "C'est pour rire." Brave soul! who doubts 'neath other skies, Before love's changeless throne: The soul that laughing, lived, and died, God welcomed as his own.
