CHESHIRE. ASSOCIATION

Distribution of Prizes

Mr: A. H. Ci ()Auld, has always taken a keNi interest in the developnatnt of the ancient game of bowls and the Lancashire and Cheshire County Bowling Association recently recognized his valuable work by electing him to the presidency. On Saturday evening the annual dinner in connection with the Association ! was held at the Palatine Hotel, Manchester, and Mr. Crosfield presided. There was a. very large and repre- sentative gathering, for in addition to a strong muster. of members of the County Association, prominent bowlers were, present froth Yorkshire, Staffordshire, Warwickshire, and other distant counties. A large contingent of Warrington devotees of the pastime was in attendance, the occasion being of special interest to them, as the Bull's Head team were to receive the handsome silver medals which they won as runiaers-up in the County Championship. Mr, Crosfield was very cordially received by the company. After dinner a smoking concert was held under the presidency of Mr. Crosfield and an admirable programme was sub- mitted. The usual toasts were honoured.

"TEE KING AND QUEEN."

The PRESIDENT said that in opening the proceedings at that very important gathering they naturally com- menced with a toast which was so popular amongst all gatherings of Englishmen that under any conditions it would be unnecessary for him to commend it to them by a speech. It was particularly unnecessary for him to do so that night because after all, popular as that toast was amongst all assemblies of Englishmen, it was perhaps received with special cordiality wherever those who were interested in British sport were gathered together. (Hear, hear.) They knew that marvellously active as the King was in so many ways—and he could assure them that his Majesty's activity at present was quite extraordinary for a man of his years—many as were the claims on his energies and attention and much as a man in his exalted position had on his mind, they knew that the King continued to take a very keen interest in British sports, and that was one reason,surely, why they should drink •,o that toast with particular enthusiasm. in proposing the toast of the King he also gave them the health of the Queen, the first lady in the land, who had held so warm a place in the hearts of our people ever since she came across the water so many years ago. (Applause.) The toast was heartily honoured. "THE PRESIDENT."

Mr. J. W. G. COOMBS, (deputy presiient) in proposing the toast of the President, said be had the honour that evening of introducing Mr. Crosfield to the members for the first time at an official gathering. His name had always been received with the enthusiasm which it deserved at their various meetings, and they were proud to have him amongst them. They trusted that would not be the last time by any means that he would preside over them. Mr. Crostleld was certainly a shining example of what could be done in one branch of sport—golf. (Applause.) They had read of the extraordinary feats he had accomplished in that beautiful country, Switzerland, and also in the British Isles. He (Mr. Coombs) was not an authority on golf but what he lacked in that respect he made up for in bowls—the most ancient of all British games. (Applause.)Mr.Crosfield had only been their president for a short period,but they had had him as a vice-president for teens of years. He (Mr. Coombs) was certain that a better man to occupy the presidential chair could not be found throughout the British Empire. (Applause.) The name of Crosfield was well-known in the com- mercial world and also in the realm of politics. A future Premiership might even be in store for him— (applause)—but at all events they trusted he would become as well known in the bowling world as he was in other walks of life. (Applause.) The toast was honoured with great enthusiasm the company singing" For he's a jolly good fellow" and giving three hearty cheers.

MR. CROSFIELD'S REPLY. HI 6 MOUNTAINEERING EXPERIENCES.

The PRESIDENT said he was very happy to have the opportunity for the first time to meet the representa- tives of bowls in Lancashire and Cheshire—indeed on that occassion they had representatives from other parts of the country, but in his remarks he would address himself more particularly to those who had had the kindness to confer upon him what he could assure them was a totally unexpected honour. He never dreamt for a moment that they had even thought of putting him in that rather responsible but very honourable and tberuestai ',ran tci nositinn—fr, /mold assuro them it was appreciated—and he thanked them very cordially. In regard to v,hat Mr. Coombs had so kindly said be could only say that he would try to live up to it. (Laughter and a Voice : " Parti- cularly the Prime Minister.") When he said that, he could assure them he was not thinking about politics. (Laughter.) No, Mr. Coombs' sugges- tion in that direction was rather too large an order, but be would t:y to live up to what he said about bowls. Whether he would succeed was another question. (Laughter.) He expected he would have to depend on their kindness to pull him through. But certainly, it was a very remarkable fact that while the game in which he took a very considerable interest—golf—had spread wonderfully in England and other countries, and while in a very small way he had tried to take his part in assisting to some little extent in its advancement, still numerically the

===== 0002601_19071102_art0090.txt | included=true | included =====
SPREAD OF BOWLS

at the present day was greater that the spread of golf— (hear, bear)—in fact it was greater than the spread of any other game he believed, under the sun. He was sure that central association hoped that progress would continue in their own counties and ultimately in all parts of the country. That was the first time in his life he bad addressed such a remarkable gathering as that. He had spoken at political meetings where there were. 3,000 people present, but it was a positive fact that at that dinner they had represented, without exaggeration, over half a million of people who were interested in the game of bowls. Ile had knocked about a bit in his time and what struck one very much as a distinguishing feature between what generally obtained i on the continent and what one found n England was the wonderful spread of athletic sports and healthy outdoor and indoor recreations in our country. Both in regard to the character and physique of our people, it was a matter for great satis- faction and even thankfulness. It'was quite astonish- ing if they came to consider how much of toil, training, and self-denial the youth of our country went through in order to achieve some proud predominance in some field of sport. He did not really know where they found the greatest sacrifices made. ft would be difficult to make a comparison between rowing, running, cycling, boxing and football. He was talking to an exception- ally good sportsman the other day—a man who told him he was willing to take on anybody in the world at 'four things—rowing, single wicket, swimming and lawn tennis—and he put it to him : " What do you consider is

THE BIGGEST PHYSICAL STRAIN

on a man in any English field of sport ?" "Well," his friend replied if you mean a test of physical endur- ance I should say that a final at Wimbledon in the singles of the lawn tennis championship is the biggest strain 'upon a man." As a matter of fact be (gr. Oros- field) should think that probably the boat race was the biggest strain for twenty minutes, unless it he a hard bit of prize fighting. (Laughter.) He rather thought, however, that in the long run, seeing how these tennis matches were dragged out, probably his friend was right and lawn tennis was one of the most severe strains one could come across. If they cline to the question of hardship and what people would undergo in the pur- suit of sport, he thought he would give them an anecdote or two from his own experience. He was not now speaking of capacity for endurance, but he believed in tie matter of hardship Englishmen under- went more outside their own country than in it. There were a certain number of lunatics who went al-road to climb mountains.(Laughter). He was afraid their presi- dent was one of them—(laughter)—and he might say that not a few of those noble patriots who served them up at Westminster—he hoped he was not patting himself on the back —(laughter)— at Christmas time tooc a little golfing holiday in the South of France. Some time back a friend of his who was one of the com- munity he had alluded to—(laughter)—said "Look here. 'Don't you go. golfing at Christmas. Come out. to Switzerland and climb." So be went. Well, he went to Chamounix and there wasa party of English people there—they were to be found wherever there was any kind of sport. They were tobogganing,skating and indulging in all the winter pastimes, so they made up a party to go up the mountain at the back of Chamounix. Unfortunately the weather had broken and there had been heavy falls of fresh snow. Mr. Crosfleld then gava a very interesting narrative of the ascent of the Montague Vert. For fire days be stayed at AN HOTEL UP IN THE MOUNTAINS, . . . .

and to giv€;.-ihenr—a-I,:idea of what it was like,- he might say that they never had the temperature higher than 10, degrees below zero—in fart it was 15 or 20 degrees below zero the whole of the time be was there, and they had a jolly, good strong wind off the glaciers and snow- fields into the bargain. If we did not not know what heat was in England, which fortunately was quite true, we did not know what cold was either. If they tried some of that mountain work in mid winter in Switzer- land, they would realize what cold was. It froze the eatables the guides had in their knapsacks stone hard— froze everything like a rock except the whisky. (Laughter). Then when they sat down to have a bite in the open they had not. to keep their feet still because there was always a chance of frost-bite catching their toes. Mr. Crosfield then described his _exciting experience in ascending one of the little aiguilles. He had two guides as fine as one could come

--OTHE'R LITTLE EXPERIENCES in a sport which was not quite so well known as bowls. He had made an acquaintance with crevasses and had stood in avalanche on one ocdasion. "Ihave told these stories in Warrington " continued Mr. Crosfield "lint I am sorry to say they are not believed. (Laughter.) They compare me with Old Tostatment characters—disadvantageously at that,—(laughter)—and I have to console myself with a Scriptural quotation that a prophet is not without honour save in his own country.' (Laughter.) So I don't think I will tell them any more Alpine tales in Warrington. I will come and tell them in Manchester. (Laughter.) lam sure if you give me the opportunity of doing so some other time you will credit me with an anxiety to avoid all improper exaggeration sad with that quality of strict veracity and attention to the • facts which you know characterises all our politicians." (Laughter.) Mr. Crosfield then tendered his sincere thanks to Mr. Coombs who since he (Mr. Crosfield) had been elected to the presidency had been exceedingly kind in assisting him. He also wished to tell the guests who had travelled from a distance how glad the members of. the Association were to welcome them. (Applause.) He hoped they were not arrogating too much to themselves hi the North if they felt that Lancashire, Yorkshire and Cheshire did, after all, form a wonderfully

IMPORTANT BOWLING CENTRE. Let them try to live up to it and as they had done very well in this part of the country, let them hold out a helping hand whenever they could to others,hoping that they might in turn become affiliated to that Association and that it might spread until old Manchester became the Marylebone of bowls. (Applause.) He could only say that during his tenure of office all he could do to help on the game would be done with a whole heart. (Applause.) Ile was very fond of golf, as they knew. It had been a hobby of his. Mr. Coombs had been good enough to refer some of his little feats in the golfing world. He was only a second class golfer, but he had had the luck to pick up a few pots. He had a cabinet at home of which he was very proud, for the prizes recalled many a good day's fighting, and he was sure the splendid prizes he would have the pleasure of distributing that evening would recall many pleasant afternoons to the recipients. One could not but feel that bowls was a time honoured game which carried them back into

OLD ENGLISH HIStOAY

—into times when there were other ports perhaps less desirable, when cock fighting bear-baiting and bull- baiting were very popular in the country. Tnese might be excellent in their way but they were not quite the sports one would like to substitute for the battles that took place between the White Rose and the Red Rose nowadays on the cricket field. (Applause). They would, he was sure, rather have the contests which were fought out in a friendly spirit between the two counties at Ohl Trafford. (Hear, hear). Above all they felt that neither cricket, golf, nor anything else was quite so

Referring to the work of the Government in Ireland Mr. Crosfield said Warrington people would hear more of that shortly because a man who was well qualified to talk about it was, he was happy to say, going to visit them. He (Mr. Crosfield) thought he had better take the opportunity when he met Mr. Birrell in Switerland, and perhaps he had to thank Mrs. Birrell for a little bit of friendly influence. Her husband was one of the hardest worked men in the House of Commons and he (Mr. Crosfield) hardly liked to press him to come to Warrington to speak. However, he had been good enough to offer to do so and he would be able to tell them something about Ireland.(Applause.) A more sympathetic, capable or broader-minded man he (Mr. Crosfield) had never met in all his political career. The Government had done something for

IRELAND already. They abolished the Coercion Act and gave grants of £l2O to £l5O to reinstated tenants. They had passed the Labourers' Cottages and Seed Potatoes Acts and they had done a little to make the battle of life easier for the very poor people who lived in Ireland. If one went to the West of Ireland, that part especially which John Morley used to call the broad fringe of wretchedness, it was rather a sad spectacle, and he really did not envy those people who only treated and always had treated Ireland as a joke or as an excuse for party tactics. It was something more serioug than that and he was glad they had so fine a man as Mr. Birrell in charge of affairs there. (Applause.) May I, proceeded Mr. Crosfield, refer to the general principle for whisk we are striving and tha efforts we are making to enforce the great objects we have in view ?—the objects of 4iving the fairest chance we can to all the people in this country, the determination that we have not to leave everything to pure individualism and luck, as Mr. Herbert Spencer in years gone by urged us to do, but that while we give all the liberty that is possible to the individual to use his special capacities and oppor- tunities we will let the State interfereas far as is necessary to give those

EQUAL OPPORTUNITIES to all our people. lam sorry to say there is a danger that in years to come this progressive programme—and it is a very progressive programme indeed that lies before us—may be checked perhaps for a d.ecade, perhaps for two decades, if a number of good but timid people in the country are frightened by another kind of gospel which is being preached very widely—a gospel which means in its real application the destruction of family life and a great cieal more which will never be tolerated in this country, and which is not toler- ated even in other countries where it would have a better chance. I will ask friends of mine—and I have good friends too who are communists—to consider, those who want to apply this gospel of communism—l use that word deliberately because, after all, they say there are 300 different kinds of Socialism—(laughter)— so that that word is apt to be just a little bit mis- leading—l ask those who want the real article, communism, to consider what has been tried in other countries ; because this is nothing new. There are countries in Europe where the conditions may not be as miserable as those I have described in India, but where they are very, very bad. Go to Italy. Go into Naples and look at the conditions of life there. Squalor and misery everywhere. A man earns 15 pence a day and he is not badly off in Naples. I have not travelled in Russia but I know a great deal by hearsay of the conditions there. I have seen some of the conditions in the Balkan Peninsular and under Turkish rule' and they are appalling. You would think that the doctrine of communism and a general dividing up of what there is to divide would succeed there if anywhere. And it does up to a point, You get communism making a certain amount of progress for instance in Italy, and you get a good deal of political power there, but only up to a point. It does not succeed even there and when you come to Britain it has no chance of doing anything whatever, except frightening a number of quiet, easy- going people away from change of any description, because if you give them the choice between this whole- sale gospel that is preached in certain quarters and re- action, they will choose reaction. Let us hope we shall have nothing so unhappy and unfortunate as that.We have bad a great deal of reaction. We have had a long spell of very reactionary, very selfish and very mischievous government. We have hand plenty of

PANDERING TO THE INTERESTS

of the privileged few and we don't want that any more. (Hear, hear.) Ido not often indulge in talk about levelling—l do not believe in it—but if you ask me frankly, I know perfectly well that the very wealthy people of this country, while they nave had to face a certain amount of graduated taxation, will have to face more, and why should they object to it? I cannot see it, and I consider that is one of the cardinal principles that we Liberals have got to reckon with. I do not think it is the most important and I do not like to put it first. No, equality of opportunity is better. Therefore I do not lay stress on the fact that there will be, and in my judgment there certainly ought to be, some further graduation of taxation, so as to put more of the burden on the shoulders of those who can bear it best, because that comes automatically. If you deal with the taxa- tion of ground values as you will have to do, it is a step in that direction, and what a crying shame it is that that question and a great many others have not been dealt with long before. (Hear, hear.) We are .twenty years in arrears with these reforms. We are a long way behind even the colonies in some respects, and won't it be a great pity if the forces of progress are ever divided up by the

RATHER RECKLESS AND THOUGHTLESS PROPIGANDA which the bulk of our community in England cannot accept? I ill give you an instance of how little the most elementary problems have been thought out by our extreme communistic friends. I mot two very capable ones in London and I said" You will admit that in your communistic community art would still be a civilising and elevating influence for the whole people ?" "Oh yes, it would " was the reply. I said " To begin with, you have old Labby 'saying we don't want any more poets, that we have got enough if people would only read them. (Laughter.) I don't think he would say the same of pictures. But let as take pictures. There are many people who think they are artists. Some of them are, and some are daubers. (Laughter.) How are you going to discrimin- ate between the two ? Herr Bebel has told us it is to be decided what every man shall do. You cannot have any freedom of choice of occupation. How are you going to settle whether a man can be an artist or not ?" " Well, by a committee "was the answer. I asked how many there would be on the committee, and they said they thought five. (Laughter.) I continued " There are artists and artists. Some are good, some are bad, and some are indifferent. How are you going to settle their remuneration? Are you going to put a Turner on the same level as a house painter? (Laughter.) Who is to settle it ?" They hesitated a bit and then said " The Committee." So you have a committee which is ' going to settle the quality of their work and I suppose ' how long they have got to work and whether they may work at all. (Laughter.) The same, of course, would apply to musicians. There are many other aspects of a , real communistic state which are not new to me. I read all this twenty years ago. It is a question which has interested rile very much and a long time since. Without going into the extraordinary positions that you get into if you really try to follow this thing out to a finish, take the most elementary problems. They won't bear a moment's investigation. "KINDRED ASSOCIATIONS."

The PRESIDENT, in proposing this toast, said he would couple with it the name of Mr. Davies, the ex-president of the Yorkshire Association. It seemed a very happy thing that whereas a few centuries back Lancashire was fighting very different battles with . Yorkshire in the days of the Red Rose and the White Rose, nowadays peaceful contests were decided on the playing fields. "My mind travels back " said Mr. Crostield "over many a season at Old Trafford, and many a historic encounter there with the county of Broad Acres. One rememhers those heroes of the bat, George Ulyett and Bates—(applause)—and many another; and some of those wonderful bowlers that Yorkshire has produced—Peel, Peate, who, all things considered, was I think at his best, the finest slow bowler; yes, perhaps the finest bowler who ever lived. And then we shall never forget Yorkshire's popular Captain, Tom Emmet. (Applause.) What a hrfiv.oniat skipper ha waa—(hefir hear)—and what a dodgy, tricky bowler too. I think I see him now tieing my cousin out at Old Traffold with one of his nasty break backs from the leg. I dare say our Yorkshire friends will agree that we have produced some useful men with both bat and ball in Lancashire too. (Cheers.) Who will ever forget that famous combination Ifornby and Barlow, who did noble service to the County through so many many years? And what about Alec Watson with the ball? Surely he, like Shaw and Peate must be reckoned in the very forefront of English bowlers. (Hear, hear.) And then in the field they were rather proud of sqme of the men in cricket annals. ,Their skipper himself was a great man at all points in the field; and then what about Frank Royle and after him Johnny Briggs at cover point? (applause)—and,if 1 may be forgiven one family allusion perhaps some of you here may agree that my cousin,who for a time captained the county, was _himself pretty useful at cover point." (Applause.) Mr. Crosfield con- cluded by saying how proud the members of the Assotiation were to be honoured with the company of so many bowler& from the various counties. (Applause). . Mr. DAVIES suitably responded. DISTRIBUTION OF PRIZES.

Before distributing the prizes, Mr. ..,i'ROSFIELD said he had seen many a fine array of prizes, but he thought those before him made about as attractive a display as ever he set eyes on. He cordially congratulated the winners—both clubs and individuals. Mr. Crosfield then handed the handsome silver championship cup, presented by himself, to Mr. W. Manion who received it on behalf of the Pendleton Belmont Club. Each member of the team received a gold medal.

Mr. CROSFIELD said there Were some other little mementos which had been won by a club that was honoured in his own native town. It gave him the greatest possible pleasure to congratulate their friends of the Bull's Head on their success in being the runners- up in that great competition. (Applause). Mr. H. FOGG received the silver medals with gold centres on behalf of the Bull's Head team and said it would not be the fault of the mtmbers if they did not win the cup next year. Mr. CROSFIELD then distributed the prizes gained in the individual merit and other competitions. The•second prize in the second division of the Association Competi- tion was won by the Warrington Cemetery team and Mr. J. Willett accepted it on their behalf.

PRESENTATION TO THE EX-TREASURER. The PRESIDENT then, on behalf of tile Association, presented an illuminated address to Mr. Robert IVilliamson (Didsbury) who resigned the treasurership twelve months ago. He said they all knew Mr. Williamson's record—in .fact he was one of the finest and most distinguished bowlers in the country. There- fore it was fitting that on laving down the burden of his duties as treasurer he should be presented with that address which he would no doubt value as a mark of the high esteem in which he was held by his brother sportsmen. He wad sure that in the whole world they would find very few Associations.which covered so great a membership and 'which therefore affected the recreation and happiness of so great a number of people as that Bowling Associa- tion. (Applause.) That enabled one to realise the res- ponsibility that devolved on the shoulders of the deputy president and the vice presidents who could do so much .to maintain the prestige of the Association and the influence of the different clubs, which went to form it. Mr. Crosfield paid'', high tribute to the ability with which these oilice'f§ discharged their duties and said Mr. Coombs was invariably courteous, cheerful and kind hearted to all who came into contact with him. ' (Applause.) ' Mr. COOMBS responded. ' At this stage of the proceedings Mr. Crosfleld left for ' Hoylake and the chair was taken by Mr. Coombs. Other toasts were honoured and a very pleasant evening ' was spent. A NATIONAL BOWLING ASSOCIATION.

An important bowling conference was held in Man- chester on Saturday, representatives attending from amateur acsociations in Lancashire, Yorkshire,Cheshire, Staffordshire, and Warwickshire. The object of the gathering wan to form a British Crown Green Bowling Association, for the purpose of setting up one standard of rules to govern all amateur bowls. Lancashire, Yorkshire, and Cheshire have .already adopted a code, and the, meeting on Saturday unanimously decided to make these universal,and stab] lab a national association. Existin,, rules already governing amateur associations were endorsed, and this means if these are to be carried out, that players will not be able to take part in any open competitions in such contests as the Blackpcol Talbot Green competition. Three representatives from each country are to be included in the woiking com- mittee, with headquarters, for the time being, at Man- chester. Mr. J. M. G. Coo-mbs and Mr. C. Smith were appointed president and hon. secretary and treasurer pro tem. A resolution was also unanimously carried that the president of each county association form the preliminary commitee.

QUALIFY INDIVIDUALTSM.

Let us recognize that mind and ability are playing, always an increasing part in the world's history, and brute force fortunately less and lesz prominent and powcrful a part than it used to have. Let us be very anxious that the qualification of individualism should go further and that it should be qualified by the development of a more active sense of social duties and social service, which is certainly being awakened in this country and in America and other civilised nations. Politics will do a great deal but when they have done all they can in the future there will remain th:- farce of altruism which will impel good men and women to help to make things better in the world. I feel most strongly and fervently how much we can do on sound and sensible lines to give the nation a better chance, to raise the national physique, to give all men a better chance of happiness, and of happiness in better ways th)n hitherto. We can do all that and we will strive for it. I hope wise counsels will prevail and that we shall not have divided forces in England wrecking the splendid opportunity that there is now in a powerful Govern- ment with an honest, courageous, and thoroughly loyal and cenonscitious chief. (Applause.)

LETTERS TO THE EDITOR.

NOTICE TO CORRESPONDENTS. Authors of letters intended for publication must in all cases send their names and addresses to the Editor a guarantee of good faith. Our Slums. Sir,—As the President of the Property Owners' Association in his address saddled the dwellers in " slum areas" with having created them, may I draw his attention to conditions which he will on reflection perceive they are not in any way responsible for : Houses crowded together. Lack of daylight and sunlight. Lick of sufficient airspace. Lack of sanitary arrangements and common decencies of civilzation. Lack of paving in courts, alleys and passages creating accumulations of filth, vermin and disease. May I also add that as the dwellers are not consulted with regard to their birthplace and as many are born where the above conditions prevail—if fortunate to survive—which many do not,then perforce they have for some years to accept their unfortunate position in life. As a rule being handicaped at the start in the race of life, they are unable to rise above these conditions, and those who are born in "slums generally remain slum dwellers. The President will perhaps pardon me for further adding that all the sophristics of human speech will fail to remove the above conditions. What is needed is that all ratepayers should assist those members of the Council who are desirous of removing the "plague spots " from our midst, and those who are most capable of rendering the assistance are the property owners-- Yours faithfully,

E. ENGLAND

===== 0002601_19071102_art0113.txt | included=true | included =====
Junior Liberals.

Dear Sir,—Permit me through the medium of your valuable paper to take this opportunity of pointing out to any young men in Warrington and neighbouring Parliamentary Divisions who are desirous of binding themselves together for the study of political questions and for active co-operation on behalf of Liberalism that full information and every assistance for enabling them to make a start can be obtained from the Lanca- shire and Cheshire Young Liberals Association. The branches affiliated to this Association are carrying out in Lancashire exactly the same work on behalf of the Liberal party that the League of Young Liberals, have carried out in London. Young Liberal branches are actively at work in Manchester, Stockport, Bolton Oldham, Livarpool, Warrington, and many other Lanca- shire towns. Since it is of such paramount importance that the present Government should be strongly supported in the determined struggle they have entered upon with the House of Lords, we strongly appeal to all young and active men to start and develop facilities for enabl- ing young Liberals to take part in this coming campaign. For any further inforarution apply F. GODWIN LLOYD.

October 30th, 1907. - Hon. Sec. L. and C. Young Liberals Association. 12, Stockport-road, Manchester.

LONDON TRAIN TRAGEDY. WARRINGTO?T-CiRPORITI9N OFFICALS' SUICIDE.

SERIOUS IRREGULARITIES IN ACCOUNTS. London, Thursday. An inquest on the body of Harold Peel Brierley, who was found dead in a lavatory compartment of a London and North-Western Railway train at Euston yesterday, was held by Deputy Coroner Shroederat the St. Pancras Coroner's Court to-day, Mr. E. Aubrey White,solicitor of Warrington,appeared to reprcsent the relatives, and the Borough Surveyor was also pre3ent. Evidence of identification was given by deceased's brother, Mr. Stanley Bryan Brierley, a designer and traveller in the cabinet trade. Deceased, he said, was confidential clerk to the Borough Surveyor of Warring- ton. He was 30 years old and married, living with his wife at Rose Cottage, Stretton, near Warrington. The Coroner : She is not here ?—No. Did she know of the inquest ?—Yes. Witness continued that his brother had been in his late position rather more than eight years. He was not a qualified surveyor. Witness last saw his brother alive last Thursday evening. He appeared well then. The Coroner: Was he a sober man ?—Yes. Temperate in all his habits.—Quite. Did he confide to you that he had any trouble ?—No. Following upon that have you received any communi- cation from him ?—No. Have you seen his wife since ?—Yes. And has she received any communication from him ? No.

_ You feel quite satisfied if she had she would have informed you of the fact ?—I am certain of it. Witness said he next heard of him on Tuesday when he heard he was missing from Warrington. Was any cause assigned ?-1 only heard the rumour that he was in trouble. What was the nature of the trouble ?—Money. Since his absence have you 'found that the rumour has any foundation ?—I am afraid it has. Do you know it ?—I don't know it authoritatively. Have you any reason to doubt it ?—No. Have you heard him threaten to take his life ? Never. George Richardson, one of the railway company's coach makers spoke to being sent to the "train to open the lavatory compartment door, which was bolted, early on Wednesday morning but before he could do so he saw the body of a man lying on the floor. Assistance was obtained and the body removed. Arthur Plagg foreman of the carriage department got through the wiudow to the deceased, and found beside him the usnal drinking glass with some white sediment in it. The man was quite dead. Ernest Savage, a train searcher, said the particular carriage in which deceased was in arrived at 4.20 on Tuesday afternoon. He could not be quite sure whether or not he searched this particular compartment. He certainly did not look in this compartment which was -known as a "dummy." It would become a " dummy "from Rugby to London. You cannot tell me then whether the deceased was in the carriage or not when the train arrived ?—I cannot. George William Peacock, a shunter, explained that " dummy " meant the t•vo compartments nearest the engine. From Warrington to Rugby there would have been carriages in front of this one. Robert Hurt, the guard of the train from Carlisle could not recognise deceased as having boarded the train at Warrington, though he could have entered the compartment in which he was found then, as it was not a "dummy "until it reached Rugby. Foreman ticket-collector Riff, at Willesden did not hear nor see anyone in the compartment. Deceased had on him a ticket for Warrington to Euston. , Dr. Maughat, who was called to the siding on which the train had been shunted on Wednesday morning, said deceased was lying face downwards against the door. His head was jammed down on to his chest. He had been dead 13 or 20 hours and an odour of the oil of bitter almonds was noticeable. Out of his pocket the police- sergeant who was with the doctor took a blue bottle in which were still about five drops. The label was that of a Warr'ngton chemist, ani was marked "poison." The cause of death was prussic acid poisoning, of which far more than a minimum fatal dose of 45 drops had been taken. Mr. E.Aubrey White (who represented the relatives) went into the box and said he had not been consulted lately. He also produced the bottle of the chemist, Ili. Ashton, of Sankey-street, Warrington, in which the poison was obtained was stated to be " dog-poisoning." Police-Sergeant Sharkall said he found on deceased among other things £l3 in gold and a cheque book. The Borough Surveyor of Warrington, Mr. Thomas Longdin was also called. Deceased had been with him nearly nine years, he said, and during that time had given every satisfaction. Up till last Tuesday morning he had done his work promptly. Witness saw him in the office then, and it was his opinion that he had left immediately after. Due of his duties was to enter up from the yard book all accounts for work done for the various committees and priva,e individuals into the Borough Surveyor's cash book. No suspicion was aroused until last week, when certain irregularities were found. It is evident that he must have had something on his mind—something which had proved to be that he was wrong in his accounts. Certain monies he had had which had not been accounted for to the Borough Treasurer. The Coroner : What is the amount ?—I cannot possibly tell, but I think it is considerable. It might amount to two or three hunndred pounds. Are you satisfied that that money had come to the hands of the deceased ?—Perfectly. Mr. Longdin added that not the slightest suspicion had been aroused in respect of the deceased. The most implicit confidence wes placed in him. No action was . taken by the Borough authorities until the Tuesday evening when a warrant was obtained. Mr. Longdin also expressed the sympathy of the authorities with Mr.Brierley's wife, mother and other relatives. Mr. Stanley Brierley, recalled, said he could in no way account for the irregularities mentioned. So far as he knew his brother did not indulge in betting, and he knew of nothing that should bring him to London. The jury returned a verdict of " Suicide when of unsound mind," and added a rider recommending that all carriages should he searched before they were turned Into " dummy "carriages.

SOCIALISM.

MR. J. HOWES' VIGOROUS CRITICISM. "A Curse to The Workingman."

The question of Socialism was debated at a meeting of the Warrington Progressive Labour League held at the Liberal Club on Tuesday evening. Mr. J. Elwell presided over a fair attendance which included several Socialists. Mr. J. MORTON introduced the question. He said he had not heard of anyone yet who had defined Socialism in a way that it could not be questioned—in fact Mr. Blatchford was apt to publish about a column of defini- tions which he found in various dictionaries. The fact was that Socialism iucluded a very large area of idealism. According to some people, Socialism was almost anything that made for progress on lines with which we had become familar in modern times, whereas on the other hand the extremist ideas included the doing away with family ties and touched the question of religion. Between the two there was room for any number of definitions, and each man could form his own idea of what the future social organization should be and have his own definition of Socialism. An oft-repeated formula was that Socialists believed in the nationalisation of the means of production, distribution and exchange. Socialists believed that a better social organisation was bound to come and so did Liberals, but they did not know—it was quite likely Socialists thought they knew and were mistaken—how it was really coming about. Mr. Morton then combatted the idea that evolution would naturally lead to Socialism and said that evolution in society as well as elsewhere was dependent on the exertions of individuals and the introduction of innovations. Dealing with municipal- isation he contended that the greater the extent to which it was carried the greater would be the liability to corruption. It was a great mistake to assume that by municipalising industries they were gradually purchasing out the capitalist. The greater municipal- isation spread and the cheaper the living could be made, the greater the number of capital industries would become and he saw no possible chance of eventually taking all from the capitalists and running the whole concern by the State. Socialists had an idea that Socialism was coming in one parti- cular direction and they were looking for the attainment of their ideas on those lines, and naturally their minds were closed to the adoption of any new idea. On the olher hand, the essential feature of Liberalism was that they had an open mind to receive new ideas and suggestions which might spread through the Liberal party until they became first class purty measures: The chief feature of Socialism was that they had formed an idea of a future state and endeavoured to work out their salvation on lines which they believed would attain that object. He maintained that no man and no body of men had any right to lay down for themselves an ideal state of that kind and endeavour to attain it. They had only to look upon the past to see the futility of any such procedure. Mr. Morton pointed out that the Socialism advocated by Fourrier about a century ago was a system of barrack life, and when that scheme was planned its supporters could have no idea of what the conditions of life would be to-day. We in our day had no more right than they had to form any idea of what might be the state of society a hundred years hence. The principle of social evolution was to improve stage by stage and leave the future to work out its own ideas.

A discussion followed in which the general feeling appeared to he that some of the objects which Socialists sought to attain were reasonable, but that the system as a whole was undesirable and impracticable. One speaker took up the cudgels on behalf of Socialism and urged that the man without an ideal was of no use. If the people did not abolish competition, the trusts would do it for them. The Socialist ideal was the nationalisation of the means of production, distribution and exchange, but they did not want to reach it in a day. They did not advocate revolution. Their methods were evolution. The CILIIRMAN agreed that it was good to have an ideal, but no man had a right to force his ideal on to other people. Socialists said that as soon as they had ' got a majority they would force everyone te accept their ideal. Advanced thought was not Socialism; it was Radicalism. Socialism did not give an opportunity for advanced thought, but would say, "Now we have attained perfection, and we are going to stop." How could there he any advancing when the Government held the means of production and distribution? Who was to be the judge as to whether an invention was of benefit or not ? If there was going to be no capital how could a man bring his invention to perfection? The more they studied Socialism the more they were convinced of its impossibility.

Mr. J. HowEs said he contended that Socialism was impossible, and that it would be the greatest detriment to the progress of the workingman if it were possible. Socialism, if it could be carried out, would curse the workingclasses of this country to a much greater extent than the worst phases of the present state. Under Socialism the Government was to control everything. In the most advanced State the people would have to elect a Government and it would control the means of production and distribution. All the great industries with all their complexities were somehow or other to be handed over to the Government. What a lively time the Government would have. (Laughter.) Every agency in the country would be presided over by a Government official, and there would thus be an enormous amount of officialism. The Government would rim all the work- shops ; therefore the people would work for the Government and every .vorkshop in the country would be a Government workshop presided over by an official. If one of the workmen offended an official and got the sack, where would he have to go for a job? There was no other workshop in the country; it was all the Government. Under the present system a man discharged from Government or muni- cipal employment could obtain work under a private employer, but under Socialism he must starve or go into another country where Socialism did not obtain. Nianicipalisation was not Socialism at all. It was the development of Radicalism which gave over to the governing bodies control of certain general things which they could best work, leaving to individual enterprise and exertion multitudes of things which the Corporation could not best do. Socialism was totally opposed to that. It wanted to abolish individual enter- prise and hand over everything. A gigantic monopoly would thus be establishad. Socialist writPrs told us that under a Socialistic State all able-bodied people would be required to work and also that they would have to work where their labour was most required. Therefore if a man who was a carpenter married a weaver, the Government inspector might decide that the husband Wa3 wanted at London and the wife at Preston. A VOICE: There would be no work for married women under Socialism. Mr. HowEs : Socialist writers say that all able- bodied people are to work. The INTERRUPTER: All able-bodied men. Oar programme includes the raising of the age at which children can work and the abolition of female labour.

Mr. HOWES : That would be an improvement on the present writings of Socialism,hut if that were so all the males would have to work where their labour was required. Therefore the father might be required in Birmingham and the son in London and the Government inspector would order them to go. There is no security for home life at all. Your own writers tell you that home life must go by default. Continuing he said that in the book " Merrie England" they were given an illustration of twenty workingmen's homes in a street, with twenty workingmen's wives cooking twenty dinners and the writer said What a waste of time and energy! How much better they would do it in a barracks where one cooks for the lot." Socialism must essentially be a barrack life and break up domestic arrangements. How would it be possible in a great empire like this for the Government to control all the means of production and distribution. What would they do with inventive genius? All the inventions had sprung from the working classes but it would be impossible under Socialism for the inventive genius of the working classes to receive encouragement, because they would have to submit their ideas to the Government Inspector. It would be such a state of slavery that no- one would endure it. There could be no empire under a Socialistic state ; it must narrow down to some little community. As soon as they got a divergence of opinion they smashed it. He opposed Socialism because it would be the greatest curse that ever came to the workingman, because it would kill the home life and destroy all domestic ties, and because it was anti- religious in the most thorough sense. It dealt only with creating a huge monopoly, and handing it over to Government officials. The curse would be so great that the workingmen would protest against it and smash it down in less than twelve months. (Applause.) After further discussion Mr. MORTON replied and said Liberalism had brought about the improvements we had to-day. Kier Hardie himself had said that Liberalism made the Labour party possible. Municipalised industries were not Socialism and never could be, as they were carried on in individualistic interests. The capitalist benefited from them in the same proportion as he did in private industries. The effort to attain ideals on the wrong lines tended to to divert energy from the right path and to retard pro- gress: The trust system instead of doing _away with competition was carrying it to extremes. He admitted that railway nationalisation had been achieved in some countries but they bad pretty much the same state of affairs that we bad, aud the same amount or even moro poverty. - -

A VICE: What about the conditions of the railway workers themselves?

Mr. Howes: They are lower.

Mr. MORTON contended that municipalised industries contributed chiefly to the benefit of the wealthy classes, just as if they were individualistic enterprises.

Great Camar's Ghost! poor wasted frame That once full vigour did possess, Can't be a man in else but name. His cough has brought him such distress. The undertaker smiles to think Next winter's cold he'll not endure; Oh! ne'er say die, but take a drink Of Wood' Great Peppermint Cure.—Advt.

Then when the Coroner was coming out of his room the Chief Constable came into the corridor and stood there expecting the Coroner would probably have something to say to him. He (Alderman Tinnion) went up and spoke to the Coroner, who referred to the treatment he got at Warrington as altogether different from that he received in other parts of Lancashire. He got better accommodation here than elsewhere. The Coroner walked past the Chief Constable —whether he saw him or not he (Alderman Tinnion) did not know, as Mr. Nicholls' back was turned to him. However' the fact remained that the Chief Constable endeavoured to have an interview with him to give an explanation. The Coroner thought fit to exercise his great powers of hearing whom he liked, and so far as the Chief Constable was concerned it was no fault of his that a full explanation was not given, either in the Coroner's Court or privately. The minutes were unanimously adopted.

ROYAL COURT THEATRE.

"THE GRIP OF IRON." This popular drama, presented by Mr. E. H. Knight's Company, has attracted large audiences to the Royal Court Theatre this week. The great feature is Mr. Fred Powell's thrilling conception of "Simmorfet, the strangler "-- a part which he has played nearly 6,000 times. A remarkably clever actor, he is the life and soul of the piece, and the many highly dramatic scenes in which he figures hold the audience spell-bound. Mr. Powell is supported by a very capable com- pany. Mr. Herbert Lewis, as Lonstalot, "the smiler" is responsible for most of the humour and all the other parts are well sustained. The play is elaborately mounted, the drawing room scene in the last act presenting a strikingly beautiful picture. NEXT WEEK'S PROGRAMME. "SWEET NANCY." The proprietors are to be congratulated upon having arranged for the visit next week of Miss Annie Hughes and her company in "Sweet Nancy" a comedy in three acts by Robert Buchanan. Miss Hughes is one of the leading actresses of the day. She has had a long experience of the stage and has played many parts, with Sir Henry Jrving, Mr. 13eeibohm Tree, Sir Charles Wyndham, in fact, with every leading actor and actor-manager in London. She has written several plays. dramatised " Lorna Doone " and produced it; "Bleak House" and not produced it ; also " Vanity Fair " (this has been produced). She has written stories for papers, and is a member of the Lyceum Club (the only actress who is). She has shown dogs, and taken many prizes. One dog presented to her by her Majesty the Queen, accompanies her on many of her journeys. Miss Hughes' chief impersonations are : " Little Lord Fauntleroy " '(the original), "Sweet Nancy," and "A Bit of old Chelsea " (the famous flower girl success), MISS ANNI_ _UGHES. while she played with Sir Henry Irving in " Waterloo "the first time he played it and the last. Miss Annie Hughes and Company are this week at the Theatre Royal, Manchester. A new play in one act from her pen—entitled "Alter many years "—will precede " Sweet Nancy " every evening at the Royal Court Theatre next week.

CHIEF CONSTABLE VINDICATED.

SUGGESTED APOLOGY FROM THE CORONER.

Manslaughter Charge Fails.

At the Warrington Police Court on Tuesday, Catherine !Kelly, 21, Morris's Court, against whom a verdict of manslaughter was returned by a Coroner's jury on October 18th in respect to the death of her thirteen months-old child, John Kelly, was brought before Messrs. H. Roberts (in the chair), W. Richardson, and C. Jackson-Holmes, on the charge that she feloniously did kill and slay the said child. The Deputy Town Clerk (Mr. A. T. Hallavi-ay) prosecuted for the police. The facts of the case were again fully gone into. Dr. Peacocke said he considered the treatment the child received hastened its death. The Chairman : Would the result of your post- mortem justify the opinion that the death of the child was quite consistent with the disease itself, apart from any other consideration? Dr. Peacocke : Yes. _

The Chairman: Apart from any knowledge you had previously of the neglect of the death of the child? Dr. Peacocke : Quite so. Dr. Harold Joseph said the child was dying when admitted to the Union Hospital. He gave a certificate that death was due to inanition and exhaustion.—ln answer to the Deputy Town Clerk, he said it was quite possible for the child to have died without any neglect at all. The Bench retired to consider the evidence, and after a brief absence, the Chairman. said: "We have con- sidered the case very carefully, and we are unanimously of opinion that the evidence is not sufficient to justify finding that there is a prima-facie case made out for manslaughter. We are also equally of opinion that the action of the society, of the police, and yourself (Mr. Hallaway) is perfectly correct in every particular, and it is a case which should have been, as it has been, fully investigated."

THE BENCH AND THE CORONER. STRONG REMARKS.

"Now the case has been disposed of," continued Mr. Roberts, "I wish to put a question to the Chief Constable. I understand this is the case to which refer- ence was made a short time ago by the Coroner, and in your absence he made some rather strong remarks to the effect that he should ask you to be present, or insist on your being present, at the adjourned inquest to explain some act of omission or commission on your part." The Chief Constable : This is the case, sir. The Chairman: Did you present yourself at the adjourned inquest for the purpose of giving the inforrua- tron that the Coroner previously said he should require from you? The Chief Constable : I was here, sir. The Chairman: Did you ask for permission to speak? The Chief Constable : I wrote to the Coroner asking him to give me an interview prior to the proceedings, but he replied to say that he thought no good ptirpose would be served.

The Chairman : Then so far as you were concerned you were practically denied by the Coroner an oppor- tunity of clearing yourself from the suggestions con- tained in his remarks at the opening of the inquest? The Chief Constable: Well, in the absence of the Coroner, I should not like to say that, sir. The Chairman: I only want to know the facts. The Chief Constable: One can only draw one's own conclusions, but in the absence of the Coroner it would hardly be proper for me to say I was denied. The Chairman: At any rate you had no opportunity? The Chief Constable: No, sir. , The Deputy Town Clerk: I think the Chief Con- stable is placed in rather an invidious position to-day in theabsence of Mr. Brighouse. But I must say this —and I think it is in common fairness to the Chief Constable—that it ought to be generally known that when the Corporation read the ex-cathedra utterances that had been made at the inquest they naturally felt very much hurt, and properly so. In fact, it was a most grave accusation, and one which made the Cor- poration think that there might be something in what had been said. If that were so it would be impossible for them to have any trust in Mr. Nicholls again, for he had been guilty of this suggested sin oeomision. In that case he was not fit to occupy the very respon- sible position which he now holds, and the Watch Committee went into the matter very carefully. After the fullest consideration of it they have passed a resolution expressing their entire confidence and satis- faction at the manner in which the Chief Constable has acted throughout in this case. I am sure the Chief Constable will be grateful to you for the expression which has fallen from your lips. The Chairman : Our feeling is this: The Chief Constable, of course, is one of the particular executive officers of the town, and we felt at the moment that unless the suggestions contained in the Coroner's remarks could be cleared up that naturally the Chief Constable, who has only just been appointed, would not have secured as promptly as we should like him to have secured, the confidence and regard of the populace of Warrington generally. We are extremely glad to know that the Watch Committee have felt it their duty to investigate the matter thoroughly, and are perfectly satisfied with his conduct. To make the whole thing complete, I would suggest that an apology is due from the Coroner to the Chief Constable for his remarks. Mr. C. Jackson Holmes: I quite agree. THE COUNCIL AND THE CHIEF CONSTABLE.

RESOLUTION OF APPROVAL. At a meeting of the Town Council on Tuesday even- ing, it was reported that the Watch Committee had resolved at their meeting on October 24th that—" The attention of this committee having been called to criticism by the Coroner for this district of the action of the Chief Constable in not reporting to him the death of a child named John Kelly, which death was duly certified by a medical practitioner, and the committee having heard the facts from the Chief Constable, it was resolved that this committee place on record an expres- sion of their entire approval of the action taken by the Chief Constable in connection with the same." When the adoption of these minutes had been moved, Councillor Lord said he would like to know if it was a fact that the Coroner refused the Chief Constable an opportunity of giving an explanation in the Court where the Coroner spoke so disrespectfully of him. He understood the Chief Constable attended the adjourned inquiry and wanted an opportunity of defending himself. The Mayor said the matter was thoroughly gone into that morning at the Borough Police Court, and the report would be in the newspapers. He repudiated any idea that the Chief Constable was to blame at all. The Chairman of the Bench that morning said that the Coroner ought to apologize to the Chief Constable. (Hear, hear.) Councillor Pemberton said what Councillor Lord asked was : "Is it true thatlhe Chief Constable asked, and was refused by the Caoner, to give an explana- tion."

The Mayor : It is not quite true, but the Chief Constable was there prepared to give an explanation if required.

Alderman Tinnion said he was present at the inquiry,

===== 0002601_19071102_art0121.txt | included=true | included =====
,- THE MAYOR THANKED.

CLOSE OF A SUCCESSFUL YEAR'S

WORK.

Tuesday evening's meeting of the Warrington Town Council was the last for the municipal year, and at the close Alder man Pierpoint moved :—" That the best thanks of this Council be presented to James Smethurst, Esq., J.P., for the satisfactory manner in which he has discharged the responsible duties devolv- ing upon him as Mayor and Chief Magistrate of the Borough during his year of office, and for the unvary- ing courtesy and impartiality displayed by him when presiding over the deliberations of the Council, and iii his intercourse with its members." He said th-re was not one word of praise embodied in the resolution that was in excess of the deserts of Alderman Smethurst, who had discharged the varied respon- sibilities of the office not only to the satisfaction of the Council but also to the satisfaction of the rate- payers whom they were all supposed to represent. (A voice : "Supposed ? " and laughter.) Since his appointment his whole desire had been to carry out the duties of his high office with impartiality and care. His constant wish had been to facilitate the manifold labours of the various officials of the Cor- poration by daily attendance at the Town Hall, and by close supervision of the proceedings of the Com- mittees to keep himself informed of the government of the town. In addition to all these admirable qualities he had been gentle and courteous in manner —not only to the members of the Council but to every official from the highest to the most humble. For all he bad done they now desired to thank him in no half-hearted manner, but with a measure of their gratitude heaped up and filled to overflowing. (Applauße.) Alderman BENNETT said he had much pleasure in seconding the resolution. He had had more than one opportunity of expressing his feelings towards the Mayor, and he had said many times that if he (the speaker) had any claim at all on the gratitude of his fellow-townsmen it was this—that at his suggestion Alderman Smethurst was made Mayor. He had never regretted having made the suggestion, as it had been justified from first to last, and he was sure that in saying that he expressed the opinions of the whole of the people of Warrington. A kindly reference was also made to the Mayoress. Everything he had said about the Mayor also applied to Mrs. Smethurst, who had rendered her husband valuable assistance in the dis- charge of his duties. (Applause.) His Worship had also been well supported by the members of his family. (Hear, hear.) Councillor EVANS said he was pleased that Alderman Bennett had referred to the Mayoress. In performing public duties a great deal of the work of a man—success or failure—depended upon the attitude his wife took on public questions generally, and he thoroughly believed the Mayoress had done all she could during , the year of office now terminating to make it as pleasant and as successful as possible. (Applause.) With regard to the Mayor, he felt personally indebted to him for the manner in which he had conducted the business of the town generally, and for the many services he had rendered in the interests of the public. He was one of the deputation appointed to ask the Mayor to accept office, and he did not regret the appointment, but looked upon it with a great deal of satisfaction. He hoped the Mayor's life would long be spared to them. (Applause.) Councillor PEMBERTON on behalf of the party with which he was associated, endorsed all that had been said. I They had been treated fairly, he said, and had always had the opportunity of expressing their views since the present Mayor had taken the chair. Councillor Dr. JOSEPH, as the seconder of Alderman Bennett's proposal that Alderman Smethurst should be made Mayor, said it would not be complete unless he i entered into the cordiality of the occasion. He agreed with every word said. Councillor CRAM said on behalf of the party he represented—the independent party—which consisted of Councillor Toole and himself—(laughter)—he thanked the Mayor for the services he had rendered. Alderman ! Smethurst had fulfilled all their expectations, and more ; than that, and he hoped he would have good health to continue the office for another year. Councillor TOOLE, on behalf of the Catholics, said he had pleasure in supporting the resolution. The Mayor had always been willing and ready to lend a helping hand for the betterment of any object that appealed to him in the town.

Alderman PIERPOINT heartily endorsed all that had been said about the Mayoress. The resolution was carried, amidst applause. The MAYOR expressed his thanks for the cOrdial and kind way in which the members had supported him in the chair. If he had been the King, they could not have benn more gentlemanly towards him than they had been—(hear,hear)—and no man's heart was more full of thankfulness than his. He had tried, with God's bless- ing he hoped, to be successful in doing his duty to the Council and to the Borough. (Hear, hear.) He heartily thanked them on behalf of his wife and family, how had taken the greatest interest in the work, and especi- ally had the Mayoress tried to fulfil her position. The Mayoress last week went through an amount of anxiety which he scarcely thought was possible. Her labours were enormous, and she was now in bed recovering from the great strain. He was sure when he told her of their appreciative remarks she would be greatly cheered. He wished to thank the Town Clerk, who had guided him successfully through the office; the Borough Surveyor, the Borough Treasurer, the Chief Constable, and especially the Magistrates' Clerk for their help during the year. He (the Mayor) believed his decision to hold office was given largely through the help the Magistrates' Clerk promised to give him on the Bench. "I heartily thank you," said the Mayor, in conclusion, "for the way in which you have supported me in this chair, and I can only say I have tried to do my duty." (Applause.)

===== 0002601_19071102_art0122.txt | included=true | included =====
NOTES ON GAMES AND

ATHLETICS.

RUGBY FOOTBALL.

By " Ovalist."

Warrington missed a fine opportunity of running well up for the Lancashire Cup. on Saturday. They certdinly to have won at Leigh, and thus quali- fied for the semi-finals. Judging by the draw War- rington would have had Broughton as opponents, which would have been a repetition of last year's history witit, I think, a different result.

The draw for the semi-finals is as follows Salford v. Oldham. Leigh v. Broughton Rangers. To be played on November 111th.

The reason Warrington did not win at Leigh CM Saturday was that they missed easy chances to score, whereas Leigh took what few opportunities came their way. It was not the case that the better team won by any means, for Warrington were a long way the superior side and ought really to have won by about 20 clear points. I never saw them attack so persistently and - fail so often at the critical moment. Isherwood himself dropped two passes which should have resulted in easy tries, but there was an excuse for his unsteadiness, in the mental anxiety he doubtless suffered. lam sure readers of this column will deeply sympathise with him in the bereavement which has E.:ince befallen Isherwood in the death of his brother.

Warrington were eight points ahead in the first quarter of an hour and looked like winning with ease. But a sad piece of bungling gave Leigh five points, and this coupled with a penalty goal quite put them on their feet again. The fatal error was made when Molyneux kicked high over the goal, and the Warring- ton men stood looking at one another while a towns- man in Gallop sc,)red a try for Leigh. There was a crowd of Warrington players behind the goal, and they had ample time to make the ball dead. A really smart try for Leigh just before the interval put them 2 points ahead.

On resuming Warrington had what advantage there was in wind and slope, and soon penned Leigh on the line. The attack was conducted with energy and skill and after Heath and Thomas had narrowly failed to score, Lees made a beautiful opening which Isher- wood had the mortification to miss almost on the line. Further passing was similarly bungled a moment later and the Leigh men nipping in quickly changed defence into attack and a long dribble resulted in another try of the soft variety. This was one of the two occasions only on which Leigh entered the Warrington quarter after half-time!

There was still time to win the game though Warrington were now five points behind. A desperate attack was kept up and Jenkins raced over, but Thomas missed a simple goal shot. Two penalties came Warrington's way also, but though he took every care, Thomas could not get the ball over the bar. In the last five minutes Warrington won, for Kenyon ran over smartly, but the referee negatived the point, thus making one of many mistakes from which both sides suffered during the afternoon. Warrington were certainly unfortunate but they brought most of the trouble on their heads themsel-c-es.

High praise is due to the Warrington forwards,who again fairly outplayed their opponents—a somewhat lighter set. Behind, Tilley kept a perfect " goal " and never once dropped. the wet ball or failed to return with power and accuracy. Dickenson, too, played a characteristic Cup-tie game and if his old partner had been in the usual steady vein, Warrington must have had an easy victory. Jenkins and Lees played well together, and the first named showed some of the form. which won him such high praise last season. Neither of the wings was a great success, though opportunities were scarce on Saturday. Leigh played very keenly but are only a poor side. Whittaker was about the best. of the backs, and Wiustanley (a forward) did excellent work in the centre after the iaterval.

On Monday Warrington managed to capture a couple of League points at the expense of Barrow. A very weak team was Put in the field, seeing that such men as Fish, Isherwood, Brooks, Taylor and Hock-enhall were all absentees. The Furness men fancied they had rather a " sweet thing " on. but they had under-estimated the strength of the Warrington reserves. Up to half time they made a close fight and were only a point behind, but in the second half the Warrington men quite over-ran them and won in the end by 5 tries to 1 goal. Thomas was again" off "as a goal-kicker and could not find the target on a single occasion.

Warrington fulfil a League engagement at Salford to-day and I am afraid they will come away pointless. Salford will be at full strength and on present form should be rather too good for Warrington's weakened forces. If Lomas were absent I should have good hopes of the Warrington men, for Weaste is a ground on which they generally give a good exhibition,except in such minor events as Cup-finals.

The Warrington team will be : Tilley, full back; Jordan, Cartwright, Dickenson and Kenyon, three- quarters; Lees and Jenkins hair-backs; Boardman, Thomas, Heath, Shugars, Harmer and Be:ton, forwards.

Brooks will be playing fqr Lancashire against York_ shire at Halifax. Fish has been suspended for a fort_ night for misconduct, and want of attention to train ing. He will have to reform if he wishes to make the acquaintance of the "All Blacks."

The latter went to St. Helens, the birthplace of their late premier, on Wednesday and easily defeated the Saints before a crewd of 7,000. The sc)re was 26-5, but the home lot should have done better with the opportunities they had. Still there was no mistaking the cleverness of the tourists. They passed through Warrington at 1.30 yesterday en route to Merthyr,whom they expect to comfortably demolish to-day.

Warrington " A" will be at home to SaHod "A" in a Combination match and ought to win. Taylor will make a welcome re-appearance, and the selected side is : Hewitt, full back ; Harrison, Lloyd, Taylor, and Harris, three-quarters ; Daintith and Tinkler, half backs; Forster, Swift, Jolley, B)ardman, Holbrook, and Bradshaw, forwards.

PENKETH.

LIBERAL CLUB. —The other evening the final of a billiard handicap took piece at the above club. The tiro opponents were Messrs Harold Chatham and Smith- Tunstall, both being scratch men, who treated the spectators to an exciting game. Mr. Chatham was the leader until about 90 Mr. Tunstall took up the lead and won by the small margin of five points. There were four prizes, the firAt being a splendid cue in case presented to the club by Messrs. Burroughs and Watts. The other gentlemen who won prizes were: 2, H. Cheetham ; 3, Frederick Dale ; I,Arthur Dewlehouse. The member- ship of the club has also improved which speaks well for the democratic cause at this corner of the Newton division.

FATAL COLLIERY EXPLOSION.

An explosion occurred at the North Seaton Pit, Northumberland, late on Friday night, causing the death of a man named Vardy, aged 23, and seriously injuring William Gregory, aged 45. rile two men were working together a considerable distance from each other when the accident occurred. Vardy, who was killed outright, was only married six weeks ago.
