MR. LYTTLETON AT WARRINGTON.

Socialism Denounced.

A mass meeting promoted by the Warrington Con- servative Association was held in the Parr Hall on Thursday evening, the principal speaker being the Right Hon. Alfred Lyttleton, M.P. There was a large attendance, Sir Gilbert Greenall presiding. The CHA.IRMA.N said it was their determination to win back the seat from the Radical party who had succeeded for a time by misrepresentation and vague promises. Warrington always had been a Tory borough and he thought everyone in the town knew that it was really and honestly a Conservative borough to-day. The Hon. A. LYrTLETON, who was cordially received, said he had heard that in the North of England—and not only in the North, but throughout England at the present time—Socialism was making headway, was propagating its issues, anl putting forth a propaganda with the greatest possible energy. (Hear, hear.) He proposed, therefore, to examine what really Socialism was—(hear, hear)—and, if possible, to endeavour to examine it with candour, and to show his hearers in what respects it failed. (Hear, hear.) What was the foundation upon which

===== 0002601_19071102_art0126.txt | included=true | included =====
SOCIALISM

as he understood it, was based? In 1891, at Norwich, the Trades Unions Congress passed a resolution to the effect that, in its opinion, there ought to be nationalisa- tion of land and all the instruments of production, distribution, and exchange. Some men in high places supported that resolution, but he thought they would , repent of that support. That resolution he took to be the meaning of Socialism as it existed in England at the present moment. The basal foundation of it was la hatred and a horror of capital. (Applause.) Capital was in the eyes of Socialists, something which exploited labour, which gave labonr merely that which was sufficient to keep it alive, and which arrested all the other productivity of labour. Another main tenet of Socialists at the present time was that manual labour was alone the source of wealth, and had alone to be considered. Brains, however, he (Mr. Lyttelton) contended had a right to their , remuneration as well as hands. (Applause.) The late Duke of Argyll was perfectly right when he said ef James Watt, the inventor of the steam engine, that the brain of Watt was the biggest source of the wages fund that he could think of in the world. (Hear, hear.) IBraincraft as well as handcraft deserved its reward as conducing to the progress of the race. (Hear, hear.) It was idle of those who tpheld the Socialistic faith to say that manual labour was alone the source and origin of wealth. Another fundamental fallacy of Socialism was that it ignored the plain facts of human nature. It .would, divest men of their strongest motive to industry. If they said to many a man that no matter whether he worked or not somebody would work, and he would enjoy part of the proceeds, he would turn away from those employments which really went to the root of the Iprosperity of the country, would become a sybarite, a loafer, and willing to live upon those who he knew perhaps might work for him. The result of dividing the instrnments of production, distribution, and of exchange equally among all people would be that that which they had to divide would become immensely smaller in a very short time. They would make a mis- take If they denied to many who held Socialistic opinions the tribute which they must pay to high aspirations and for a generous disposition towards the poor and the miserable. (Elear, hear.) Many of those who advocated Socialism, however soft their heads might be, their hearts were in the right ploce. There were, however' many Socialists who con- stituted the driving force of the party who held \opinions both with regard to religion and morals which would be destructive of human society. The doctrines of Socialism preached at every street corner demanded attention, and demanded theix reason. They had to give the reasons, as Conservatives, for the faith that was in them, and to endeavour to drive those who held such opinions from the attitude they had taken up, for those opinions were demoralis- ing to those who at present held property. What was to be desired was that those who owned property should deal with it generously, but when the existence even of that for which they had laboured was threatened the natural disposition of the owner of property in the presence of such doctrines was to make him think it was desirable to keep what he had for himself and his family while he might. Nothing could be more disastrous to a community, and it was for that reason he had troubled the meeting at such a length upon the topic. The history of their party in the past showed they had in the past had the perception to recognise the great

===== 0002601_19071102_art0127.txt | included=true | included =====
PROBLEMS Olt SOCIAL POVERTY

and to deal withthem. More thansixty years ago Lord Beaconsfield. in his great novel" Sybil,' presented the social ills of this country in a manner which must have burned itself into the recollection of any who had read it. Throughout his life Lord Beaconsfield was an adherent of social reform. No one painted with more vividness the sufferings of the poor; no one addressed himself with more wisdom to the remedy, and that had buou followed up by Lord Sandbar)? ana Mr. Balfour, and last but not least, by Mr. Chamberlain. (Applause.) The reforms which these great men had put into force might well fall into the category of those recommended by Mr. John Morley last week in a great speech on India, when he said : "Be reformers, but be slow reformers. Have indomitable patience, but above all be firm." When they thought of the great reforms of the Conservative party, of the laws which freed ethe com- binations of labour in this country, of the workmen's compensation in dangerous trades, of the factory laws, of free education, or artisans' dwellings, and of public health acts, they would recognise that the history of the Unionist party had been one with respect to social reform of which no-one who belonged to that party need be ashamed. (Applause.) When they heard people say "We are all iocialists nowadays, wise.men meant that when the ground had been thoroughly well explored, and the future assured of such undertakings as he had mentiotied, that then was the time for the community to come in and buy on fair terms from the pioneers the private individuals and private companies who ha4d borne the heat and racket of the day, to be used thereafter for the benefit of the community, those industries and opportunities -which were really pushed forward by the existence and the prosperity of the community. (Applause.) There was a necessity in all those cases, unfortunately, as there was in most cases in life, for money. He did not think that anybody who studied the present system of taxa- tion would believe that much more could be laid either upon the upper or the middle classes than at present existed. The Unionist party, whilst desirous above all things of pushing forward with caution social reform, admitted to itself that there would be necessary for such social reform some broadening of the

PASTS OF PRESENT TAXATION. (Applause.) It was hardly possible to conceive that without tariff reform of some kind or another that that which was absolutely certain to come would be able to be paiJ for. (Applause.) The desire of the Unionist party—with some dissentients whom they must reason with, and as to whom he would never be a party to driving them out—the desire of the Unionist party as a whole, was to adjust the Customs of this country in such a manner as to bring, at any rate, the foreigner into partnership with us in some of the burdens we had to bear. (Applause.) It was the aim of the Unionist party. in the first place not to neglect the several reforms of which ho had spoken, to make employment less precarious and less uncertain than at present, and to derive some part of the burdens of the taxpayers from those foreign nations which enjoyed the immense advantage of our markets. It was part of the same system to recognise aud take means for securing the commercial unity of those splendid and growing States (the Colonies), without whose custom, even at the present momeni, we should be far less well off than we are, and with whose aid in the future, freely given under a system of preference, we would undoubtedly maintain, and more than maintain the position of pre-eminence which we once held in the commercial world. (Applause.) He believed that those great objects were worthy of the aspirations of a great party ; they were worthy of their strongest endeavour and sacrifices. As such he commended them to his hearers, without a doubt that they would feel that a party which had that policy was one worth belonging to and in whose future they might believe and have full confidence. (Applause.) On the motion of Mr. W. P. RYLANDS seconded by Mr. ALLAN SYKES (prospective candidate for the Knutsford Division) and supported by Mr. R. PIER- POINT, a resolution was passed conveying thanks to Mr. Lyttleton for his speech, and expressing unshaken eon- lidence in the Unionist leaders.
